
⏺ Yes, and I've taken it out. It would have gained little for T6 and cost the
  transaction you have open.

  Why it gains little

  Partitioning helps a query skip data it would otherwise read. T6 no longer
  reads that data: the covering index from expt_b3 already limits each shard to
  the entries inside the 30-day window. With monthly partitions on top, each
  shard would read the same roughly 13,000 entries, just out of two smaller
  indexes.

  Partitioning would have paid off before expt_b3, when T6 scanned whole tables.
  It is the alternative to the index, not an addition to it.

  What it would cost

  T5 updates by order_id alone, so each update would have to look in every
  monthly partition of the shard, about 16 of them. The same applies to T1's
  final update of the order total, and T3 would search every partition for a
  customer's orders. Together those are a quarter of the mix, and T5 is
  currently one of the cheapest transactions at 1.4 ms.

  I haven't measured either side; this is reasoning from how the queries are
  written.

  What I changed

  - run.sh in expt_b4 is back to the expt_b3 schema. The only difference from
    expt_b3's run.sh is now the plan capture at lines 516-532
    (expt_b4/run.sh#L516-L532).
  - The literal T6 window and the three read-only declarations are untouched.
  - expt_b4/README.md is updated, with partitioning recorded under "considered
    and left out" and the reasons.

  expt_b4 now carries two behaviour changes that can be told apart: the abort
  counts isolate the read-only declarations, and T6's latency together with the
  plan file isolates the literal window.

  If the captured plan shows T6 is not using the index as intended, partitioning
  becomes worth a run of its own. The short 6-minute submission is still how
  I'd start, since the T6 rewrite is untested.

⏺ The read-only declarations fixed the client aborts: 2 across the measured
  runs, against 41 in expt_b3. The speed comparison is not clean, because this
  job (931181) landed on mixed nodes: the coordinator and one worker on xcne,
  three workers on xcnf.

  Critical stats and Results are written into expt_b4/README.md.

  Aborts and retries

  - The flag does reach the workers. The worker logs don't record BEGIN, but the
    cancellation reason that a read-only reader suppresses fell from 700 to 16.
  - T6 now takes the cancellations instead. About 1% of T6s are retried at 64
    clients, and none failed.

  Throughput

  ┌─────────┬─────────────┬─────────────┐
  │ Clients │ expt_b3 TPS │ expt_b4 TPS │
  ├─────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 1       │ 97.9        │ 66.8        │
  ├─────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤

  ├─────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤

  │ 16      │ 231         │ 270         │
  ├─────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 32      │ 232         │ 298         │
  ├─────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 64      │ 249         │ 316         │
  └─────────┴─────────────┴─────────────┘

  - At 1 client everything is slower, which is the hardware. T3's query is
    unchanged and went from 10.9 to 19.4 ms; T6 went from 65 to 97.5 ms.
  - From 8 clients up it is 13 to 28% higher, but I can't say how much of that
    is the change.
  - The literal window's effect on T6 can't be read from this run.

  What the plans show

  - T6's plan has no defect left. Each shard does an index-only scan with zero
    table lookups, reads about 12,800 rows, and takes about 20 ms. Planning is
    about a tenth of that.
  - The cost is the work itself: about 410,000 rows read and grouped per T6.
    Settings such as shard count can only trim the planning share.
  - T3 is wasteful. Each of the 32 shards reads every order of the merchant (727
    rows on 571 pages for merchant 1) and then discards all but the customer's.
    No index covers customer_id.

  T6 slows down during a run

  At 16 clients, with all clients running, T6's average rose from 306 ms in the
  first 120 s to 424 ms in the last. T3, T7, T1 and T2 stayed flat, and
  throughput fell from 306 to 239 TPS.

  The likely cause is the wear-off listed as a side effect in expt_b3: T5 and T1
  un-mark the pages they touch, so T6 goes back to the table for a growing
  share of rows. This is not measured, because the plan was captured before any
  benchmark ran.

  This contradicts two lines in expt_b3/README.md, which I have not edited:

  - "No wear-off within a run" holds only at 1 client.
  - The fall in throughput at 64 clients was put down to aborting clients alone.

  Next step

  I'd make expt_b5 two small things:

  1. An index on orders(merchant_id, customer_id) for T3. T3 is 10% of all time
     and competes with T6 for the same workers.
  2. Capture the T6 plan again after each measured run. That confirms or rules
     out the wear-off before we pick a fix for it.

  The larger T6 question (a precomputed rollup, or keeping T5's updates off the
  pages T6 reads) is better decided once the second item reports back. I'll set
  up expt_b5 when you give the go-ahead.

       49 -pgbench is closed-loop: each client waits for its transaction
          -to finish before sending the next. Throughput is therefore the
          - number of clients divided by the weighted mean latency. A slo
          -w transaction with a small weight can cost more than a fast on
          -e with a large weight.

       84 +### What the runs showed beyond their own change
       85
       68 -Under SERIALIZABLE, a sequential scan takes a predicate lock o
          -n the whole shard table. T7's scans therefore conflict with ev
          -ery concurrent inventory write (T1, T2, other T7s). Most basel
          -ine serialization failures should trace back to T7, and should
          - disappear once the scans do (**Expected**).
       86 +- **Rank by share of time, not by weight.** pgbench is closed-
          +loop, so a slow transaction with a small weight holds clients
          +longest. T3 at 5% of the mix took 68% of the time.
       87 +- **The workers are the bottleneck, not the coordinator.** Fro
          +m 1 to 64 clients in `expt_b3`, `BEGIN` went from 0.06 to 0.36
          + ms, while statements that reach the workers slowed 5 to 12 ti
          +mes.
       88 +- **A round trip to the coordinator costs about 0.06 ms** at 1
          + client (the `BEGIN` line). Removing round trips alone saves l
          +ittle; the saving has to come from fewer statements sent to wo
          +rkers.
       89 +- **Two-phase commit is cheap on its own.** At 1 client in `ex
          +pt_b3`, T7's two-phase commit took 1.5 ms against 0.55 ms for
          +a one-worker commit.
       90 +- **Citus reports a serialization failure at a one-worker `COM
          +MIT` as a generic error**, so pgbench does not retry it and st
          +ops that client. The same failure at `PREPARE TRANSACTION` is
          +retried. Declaring the readers read-only removed almost all of
          + both.
       91 +- **Node types differ a lot.** A one-shard commit took 0.38 ms
          + on `xcnf` and 5.4 ms on `xcnc`. Speeds compare only between r
          +uns on the same nodes.

       72 -All **Read** from the loader in [run.sh:459-499](../expt_b0/ru
          -n.sh#L459-L499).
       95 +### Current design
       96
       74 -- **`inventory_id` equals `product_id`** for every row ([run.s
          -h:474](../expt_b0/run.sh#L474)). The provided T1 and T2 alread
          -y rely on this.
       75 -- **A product's merchant is `1 + (product_id - 1) % NUM_MERCHA
          -NTS`**, and the same for customers ([run.sh:470-472](../expt_b
          -0/run.sh#L470-L472)). The provided T9 computes it this way ([T
          -9_update_product.sql:9](../expt_b0/transactions/T9_update_prod
          -uct.sql#L9)).
       76 -- **An order's merchant cannot be computed from its id.** It c
          -omes from a hash, skewed so that 10% of merchants get 50% of o
          -rders ([run.sh:478-485](../expt_b0/run.sh#L478-L485)).
       77 -- **Every loaded order has exactly 3 items.**
       78 -- **Loaded orders have only 365 distinct `created_at` values**
          -, the load time minus 0 to 364 days, spread evenly through the
          - table ([run.sh:485](../expt_b0/run.sh#L485)). T6's window cov
          -ers 30 of them, about 411K orders, plus every order placed dur
          -ing the run.
       79 -- **The load writes every order twice.** It inserts with a zer
          -o total and then updates all 5M rows ([run.sh:495](../expt_b0/
          -run.sh#L495)), leaving one dead version per order. No `VACUUM`
          - follows.
       80 -- **Skew is mild for B.** Only T1 to T4 pick hot merchants (30
          -% of B). T7 and T9 pick products uniformly, T8 picks merchants
          - uniformly, and T5 picks uniformly among the loaded orders onl
          -y.

      128 +- **T6 slows during a run under load.** At 16 clients its aver
          +age rose from 306 ms in the first 120 s to 424 ms in the last,
          + while the other transactions stayed flat. Suspected cause: T5
          + and T1 un-mark the pages they touch, so T6's index-only scan
          +goes back to the table for more and more rows. Not confirmed.
      129 +- **The literal window's effect on T6 is unmeasured**, because
          + `expt_b4` ran on different nodes from `expt_b3`.
      130 +- **`expt_b1` has no run on `xcnf` nodes**, so its own contrib
          +ution is not measured like-for-like.
      131 +- **Two clients still abort per job**, and about 1% of T6s are
          + retried at 64 clients.
      132
      102 -- **Proposal:** option a as the main hypothesis, option b as i
          -ts control. They differ only in whether the tables are spread,
          - so the pair isolates the cost of T7's two-phase commit.
      103 -- **Why a:** T7 has three times T1's weight. The rows are smal
          -l and indexed, so the coordinator should saturate before that
          -one worker does (**Expected**). Since each experiment sets up
          -and tears down its own database, there is no rebalancing or gr
          -owth to plan for.
      104 -- **Risk:** the single worker saturating at high client counts
          -. Watch its CPU and the T7 latency curve.
      105 -- **Not an option:** reference tables. A write to one is appli
          -ed on every node, and 60% of B writes to `products` or `invent
          -ory`.

      121 -#### A4. Shard count — Open
      152 +#### C2 in detail
      153
      123 -- **What:** set `citus.shard_count` lower than 32, for example
          - 8 or 4.
      124 -- **Why:** routed queries do not care about shard count, but e
          -very fan-out does. T6 is 10% of B and always reaches every sha
          -rd, so fewer shards means fewer tasks and connections per T6 (
          -**Expected**).
      125 -- **Cost:** less parallelism inside one T6. Worth trying two o
          -r three values.
      154 +- **Why:** it is the "pre-aggregate with rollups" strategy in
          +[citus.md](citus.md). T6 would read about 30,000 rollup rows i
          +nstead of 410,000 orders.
      155 +- **Cost 1:** it must reproduce T6's exact window, `[now - 30
          +days, now)`. Whole days come from the rollup; the partial day
          +at each end has to come from `orders`.
      156 +- **Cost 2:** two concurrent orders for the same merchant on t
          +he same day update the same rollup row, and under SERIALIZABLE
          + the second fails and retries. Expected to be rare: the busies
          +t merchants each take 0.5% of orders.
      157 +- **Cost 3:** T1 writes to one more shard.
      158 +- **Check first:** whether a pre-aggregated table is an accept
          +able optimization. See Open questions.

      181 -#### C1. `VACUUM` after load, plus a covering index — Open
      223 +### Measurements still owed
      224
      183 -- **What:** an index on `orders(created_at) INCLUDE (merchant_
          -id, total_amount)` and a `VACUUM` at the end of the load.
      184 -- **Why a covering index:** an ordinary or BRIN index on `crea
          -ted_at` will not help much. With 365 timestamps spread evenly,
          - almost every page holds rows in the window, so the scan still
          - touches the whole table. A covering index lets the scan read
          -8% of the index and skip the table (**Expected**).
      185 -- **Why `VACUUM`:** an index-only scan skips the table only fo
          -r pages marked all-visible, and the load leaves none marked. I
          -t also clears the 5M dead versions.
      186 -- **Risk:** T5's updates un-mark pages during the run, so the
          -benefit may shrink over the 600 seconds. Compare T6 latency ea
          -rly and late in the run.
      187 -- **Side effect:** T1 updates `total_amount` after inserting t
          -he order. With the column in an index, that update can no long
          -er be a heap-only update. B3 can avoid it by inserting the ord
          -er with its final total.
      225 +These are not new ideas; they close gaps in what has been run.
      226

          + rule out table lookups as the cause of the slowdown. Proposed

          -r day, built from the loaded orders and kept current by T1. T6
          - reads about 30 rows per merchant.
      192 -- **Why:** it removes the orders scan from T6 entirely. This i
          -s the "pre-aggregate with rollups" strategy in [citus.md](citu
          -s.md).
      193 -- **Cost 1:** it must reproduce T6's exact window, `[now - 30
          -days, now)`. Whole days come from the rollup; the partial day
          -at each end has to come from `orders`.
      194 -- **Cost 2:** two concurrent orders for the same merchant upda
          -te the same rollup row. Under SERIALIZABLE the second one fail
          -s and retries.
      195 -- **Check:** whether a pre-aggregated table is an acceptable o
          -ptimization. See Open questions.

      295 +- **Loaded orders have only 365 distinct `created_at` values**
          +, the load time minus 0 to 364 days, spread evenly through the
          + table. T6's window covers 30 of them, about 410,000 orders, p
          +lus every order placed during the run.

          +o total and then updates all 5M rows, leaving one dead version
          + per order. Since `expt_b3` a `VACUUM` clears them.
      297 +- **Skew is mild for B.** Only T1 to T4 pick hot merchants (30
          +% of B). T7 and T9 pick products uniformly, T8 picks merchants
          + uniformly, and T5 picks uniformly among the loaded orders onl
          +y.

⏺ docs/workload_b_proposal.md is reorganised around milestones and remaining
  untested ideas.

  New structure

  1. Summary: where we are (3.5 to about 250 TPS on the same nodes), what the
     ceiling is (T6), and how the plan departed from the first version.
  2. The workload: the transaction mix table, unchanged.
  3. Milestones: one row per experiment from expt_b0 to expt_b4, with throughput
     and 1-client latency across all five and the lessons that cut across runs.
  4. Where things stand: the current design, where the time goes at 16 clients,
     and known problems.
  5. Remaining untested ideas: grouped by the transaction they help, in order of
     share of time (T6, T7, T3, T1, then all transactions).
  6. Suggested order, Considered and set aside, Grey areas and open questions.
  7. Background: how an experiment runs, facts about the data, implementation
     constraints.

  What changed in substance

  - Old idea codes are kept (A1, B2, C2 and so on), so earlier references still
    resolve.
  - Ideas from our discussion that were never in the doc are added: the
    materialized view, moving order_status to its own table, more frequent
    autovacuum, READ ONLY DEFERRABLE, slow start, and dropping inventory_id as
    cleanup.
  - Some expectations are revised by what the runs showed. For example, removing
    client round trips is now rated as a small gain, because a round trip to
    the coordinator measures about 0.06 ms.
  - A "Measurements still owed" list records the gaps: expt_b1 on xcnf nodes,
    expt_b3 against expt_b4 on the same nodes, and the index without VACUUM.
  - The "Read" and "Expected" tags are gone. A line at the top says milestones
    are measured and ideas are expectations unless they cite a run.
  - The old "Proposed order of experiments" table is replaced by the milestones
    table and a four-step suggested order.

  The detailed option tables for A2 and A3 are condensed into rows of the idea
  tables. The B2 SQL sketch and the rollup's costs are kept in full.

  expt_b5 appears in the doc as proposed and not built. The two lines in
  expt_b3/README.md that expt_b4 contradicts are still unedited.

❯ should we do something simple next?
  1. add index for customer_id (primary key should auto have it?), merchant_id
  (primary key should auto have it?), products(product_id, merchant_id),
  orders(merchant_id, customer_id)
  2. use uuid7() for *_id keys (but i am not sure if this will break the
  script)

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes to something simple, but only one of those four indexes would do anything,
  and I'd skip the UUIDs.

  1. Indexes

  ┌─────────────────────────┬─────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Index          │ Verdict │                Why                 │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ customers(customer_id)  │ Already │ The primary key creates it. T8     │
  │                         │  exists │ finds its row through it.          │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                         │ Already │ The baseline creates               │
  │ merchant_id             │  exists │ idx_<table>_merchant_id on all     │
  │                         │         │ five tables.                       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                         │         │ The primary key on product_id      │
  │ products(product_id,    │ No gain │ already lands on exactly one row,  │
  │ merchant_id)            │         │ and the merchant_id check is then  │
  │                         │         │ made on that row.                  │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ orders(merchant_id,     │ Worth   │ This is the T3 index. It is the    │
  │ customer_id)            │ doing   │ only one with measured waste       │
  │                         │         │ behind it.                         │
  └─────────────────────────┴─────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  - T8 and T9 have nothing to recover. They take about 1.5 ms at 1 client, of
    which 0.55 ms is the commit, and 1.3% of all time each.
  - T3 does. Each of 32 shards reads all of the merchant's orders (727 rows on
    571 pages for merchant 1) and discards all but the customer's. T3 is 10.4%
    of all time.
  - Your two-column version is enough. A customer has about 5 orders spread over
    32 shards, so adding created_at to the index would sort almost nothing. The
    proposal doc lists the three-column version; I have left it as is.

  One follow-on, not for the same experiment: once that index exists,
  idx_orders_merchant_id is redundant, since the new index starts with the same
  column. Dropping it would give T1's insert one index fewer to maintain.

  2. UUIDs for the id columns

  I'd leave this out. It would break the scripts and I don't expect a gain.

  What breaks:

  - Every script picks its ids with integer arithmetic. For example, T8 computes
    customer_id as merchant_id + slot * NUM_MERCHANTS, T9 computes the merchant
    as 1 + (product_id - 1) % NUM_MERCHANTS, and T5 picks random(1,
    INITIAL_NUM_ORDERS).
  - pgbench cannot pick a random existing UUID without first running a lookup
    query, which adds a statement to every transaction.
  - The loader builds ids with generate_series and relies on inventory_id =
    product_id.

  Why no gain:

  - UUIDv7 solves a problem we don't have. It lets many nodes generate ids
    without coordination. Here only T1 generates ids, from a sequence on the
    coordinator, at 0.16 ms per call.
  - Shard balance doesn't depend on the id's shape. Citus hashes the
    distribution column either way.
  - The keys get bigger: 16 bytes against 8, in every primary key and index.

  Next step

  expt_b5 would then be expt_b4 plus one line: the index on orders(merchant_id,
  customer_id). I'd also add the plan capture after each measured run, since it
  is diagnostic only and tells us why T6 slows down under load. Say the word and
  I'll set it up; tell me if you'd rather leave the capture out.

❯ who added this idx_orders_merchant_id?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Nobody on our side: it comes with the provided baseline. The same line sits at
  line 433 of run.sh in both expt_a0 (expt_a0/run.sh#L433) and expt_b0
  (expt_b0/run.sh#L433), and git traces it to the initial commit (d390760,
  2026-10-09). Every later experiment inherited it by copying.

  The baseline creates five of these, one per table: idx_customers_merchant_id,
  idx_products_merchant_id, idx_inventory_merchant_id, idx_orders_merchant_id
  and idx_order_items_merchant_id.

  Up to expt_b4, idx_orders_merchant_id was the index T3 used to find a
  merchant's orders on each shard. In expt_b5 the new orders(merchant_id,
  customer_id) index should take over that job, which is why I called the old
  one redundant.

❯ what about stored functions ?

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Stored functions are allowed, and they are already in the list under other
  names: "rewrite T7 without the lookups" and "T1 as one function call" would
  both be built as functions. project.md (docs/project.md) names stored
  procedures as an example of permitted transaction rewrites. With the current
  placement the gain is modest, because no transaction qualifies for the kind of
  function that saves the most.

  Two kinds, with different savings

  Kind: Function on the coordinator
  What it saves: Round trips between pgbench and the coordinator, about 0.06 ms
  each at 1 client. Every statement inside still travels to a worker.
  Requirement: None
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Kind: Distributed function (create_distributed_function)
  What it saves: Round trips between coordinator and worker, 0.4 to 1.2 ms each
  at 1 client. The whole call runs on one worker.
  Requirement: Everything the function touches must be on the shard its argument

  routes to

  Which transactions could use one

  - T2, T3, T4, T5, T6, T8, T9: each is already a single statement, so there is
    nothing to bundle.
  - T7 (30% of the mix, 12.4% of time): its two products are on different shards
    31 times in 32, so it can only be a coordinator function. The gain comes
    from dropping the four lookups inside it, about 2 of T7's 5 ms at 1 client.
    The saved client round trips add about 0.4 ms.
  - T1 (10%, 8.8% of time): it touches customers, up to five products, and the
    order, which sit on up to seven shards. That also means a coordinator
    function only, so its roughly 25 statements still go to the workers one by
    one.

  When the second kind would pay off

  A distributed function needs T1's tables on one shard, which means putting
  customers, products, inventory, orders and order_items all on merchant_id. T1
  would then be one call on one worker with a one-worker commit.

  That is a full redesign with costs elsewhere: T5 loses its routing key, and T7
  still spans two merchants. The notes in citus.md (docs/citus.md) record only
  a 3 to 6% gain from this on a single machine, where hops are nearly free; ours
  cost more, so the gain should be larger, but I have no measurement.

  Where it fits

  Neither kind touches T6, which is still 62.5% of the time. I'd treat the T7
  function as the next candidate after expt_b5, since it needs no placement
  change. The main risk is correctness: pgbench stops a client on any error
  other than a serialization or deadlock failure, so the function must never
  raise one, and it must leave the source row untouched when the destination is
  missing.

  Separately, on the earlier UUID question: project.md (docs/project.md) lists
  changing how ids are produced as a grey area, which is one more reason to
  leave it.

❯ i think it does makes sense since the same order-items of an order would
  belong to the same merchant. all three would colocate and supersedes expt_b2.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Your reasoning holds: an order and its items always share a merchant, so
  customers, orders and order_items can form one co-location group on
  merchant_id. It does supersede expt_b2, and it keeps that experiment's gain,
  because the join in T3 still happens inside one shard. The cost is T5, and it
  has to be handled in the same experiment.

  What it buys

  - T3 becomes one task instead of 32. It carries both merchant_id and
    customer_id, so it routes to one shard. This is the main gain.
  - T6 gets slightly cheaper on the coordinator. Grouping is then on the
    distribution column, so each shard can return its own top 20. That saves
    about 10 ms of 90 at 1 client; the work on the shards is unchanged.
  - T8 stays routed, since it already filters on merchant_id.
  - T1 gains nothing from this alone. Its order and items already share a shard
    since expt_b2, and its products are still elsewhere.

  What it costs: T5

  T5 updates by order_id only. Once orders is on merchant_id, that update goes
  to all 32 shards and commits in two phases across all four workers. T5 is 10%
  of the mix, twice T3's weight, so left alone it would give back more than T3
  gains.

  The fix is the mapping table from order_id to merchant_id. T5 then looks up
  the merchant and updates with both keys, two routed statements instead of one.
  T1 has to insert into that table, which adds one more shard to its commit.

  What has to change

  - Primary keys must include merchant_id: customers(merchant_id, customer_id),
    orders(merchant_id, order_id), order_items(merchant_id, order_id,
    order_item_id).
  - T3's join needs AND oi.merchant_id = o.merchant_id, so Citus can see both
    sides are on one shard.
  - T1's final update of the total filters on order_id only and needs
    merchant_id added.
  - T5 gets the lookup, and T1 the extra insert.
  - The loader's last statement, which fills in order totals, joins orders to
    order_items on order_id alone. It needs merchant_id in the grouping and the
    join, or Citus cannot run it shard by shard. I haven't tested this, and it
    is the first time we would touch the loader. The rows it produces stay the
    same.

  How much it is worth

  That depends on expt_b5. The index there already cuts T3's work per shard, so
  this change would only remove what is left: 32 small tasks and their planning.
  If expt_b5 shows T3 still costing several milliseconds, this is worth doing.

  The larger payoff comes one step further. T1 picks products belonging to the
  order's own merchant, so with products and inventory on merchant_id as well,
  all of T1 would sit on one shard. That gives T1 a one-worker commit and makes
  the distributed function possible.

  I'd build this as expt_b6 once expt_b5 is back, with the mapping table
  included so T5 doesn't regress.

❯ <ide_selection>The user selected the lines 14 to 14 from

  order_id

❯ so we need to create a new mapping table order_id -> merchant_id?

⏺ Yes, if orders moves to merchant_id. It isn't needed today, because T5 routes
  on order_id directly; the table is the price of that move.

  What it would look like

  CREATE TABLE order_merchant(order_id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY, merchant_id BIGINT
  NOT NULL);
  SELECT create_distributed_table('order_merchant', 'order_id');

  T5 becomes a lookup followed by the update, both routed:

  BEGIN;
  SELECT merchant_id AS order_merchant_id FROM order_merchant
  WHERE order_id = (:order_id)::bigint \gset
  UPDATE orders SET order_status = 'SHIPPED'::text
  WHERE merchant_id = (:order_merchant_id)::bigint AND order_id =
  (:order_id)::bigint;
  COMMIT;

  Three other places change:

  - The loader fills the table from orders after loading them, 5M small rows on
    every reload.
  - The TRUNCATE in the loader must include the new table.
  - T1 inserts one row into it for each new order.

  What it costs

  - T5 goes from one statement to two. I'd expect roughly 0.5 to 0.9 ms on top
    of its 1.5 ms at 1 client, going by what other routed lookups cost.
  - T1 writes to one more shard, usually on a different worker from the order
    itself.
  - The load gets longer by one 5M-row insert. It is about 2 minutes now.

  Nothing here is tested. One thing I'd verify in the first run: T5's lookup and
  update will usually land on different workers, and I expect a one-worker
  commit since only one of them is written.

  The alternatives are worse

  - Leave T5 to reach all 32 shards. It would need an extra index on
    orders(order_id), and every status update would commit in two phases across
    all four workers. T5 is 10% of the mix.
  - Make the mapping a reference table. Every T1 would then write to all four
    workers.
  - Compute the merchant from the order id in the script. The loader derives it
    from a hash, so T5 could repeat that formula. It would only hold for loaded
    orders, not ones T1 creates, which makes it hard to defend as a design.

⏺ The index worked, and the run answered both questions: T3's per-shard work is
  now near zero, and T6's slowdown under load is confirmed as table lookups.
  This job (931635) ran on xcnf[8-12], the same nodes as expt_b3, so those two
  compare directly; expt_b4 ran on mixed nodes and does not.

  Critical stats and Results are in expt_b5/README.md.

  T3 is still slow under load even though its work is gone. Its 32 tasks need
  under 1 ms each, yet it takes 602 ms at 64 clients, while T4, a single routed
  task, takes 20 ms at the same load. What is left is the cost of reaching 32
  shards on busy workers.

  That is direct support for your idea of moving orders and order_items to
  merchant_id: it would turn T3 into one task like T4.

  T6

  The plan captured after each run shows the table lookups growing:

  - About a third of the rows end up needing a table lookup, and the task takes
    about 40% longer. T6's latency at 16 clients rose from 301 to 439 ms within
    the run, which matches.
  - Most of it traces to T5, by my estimate. New orders account for about 600 of
    the lookups per shard. The other 4,000 are loaded orders on pages that were
    modified, and only T5 modifies loaded orders.

  - This is the combined effect of the read-only declarations, the literal
    window, and the index. The run cannot split it.
  - Commit times vary between runs on the same nodes (0.30 ms here against 0.55
    ms in expt_b3), which accounts for some of the 1-client difference.
  - Two clients aborted in the measured runs, the same as expt_b4.

  What next

  There are two targets of similar size:

  1. orders and order_items on merchant_id, with the mapping table for T5. This
     removes most of T3's time, which is 7% of the total at 16 clients and 15%
     at 64.
  2. order_status in its own table. This stops T5 un-marking the pages T6 reads.
     T6 would stay near its start-of-run cost, which I estimate at about 12%
     more throughput at 16 clients.

  The two interact. With the status in its own table on order_id, T5 would not
  need the mapping table, but T3 would have to fetch the status from a table
  that is not co-located with orders. It is worth deciding on them together.

  I'd take the first as expt_b6, since the T3 evidence now backs it. Say which
  you want and I'll build it.

  The proposal doc still describes expt_b5 as not built and the T6 cause as
  unconfirmed; I've left it unchanged.

❯ okay summarise of all the changes per experiement and the
  results/observations we glean and the rationale why we did the change. per
  expt in a table

⏺ Six experiments so far, each built on the one before. expt_b0, expt_b2,
  expt_b3 and expt_b5 ran on the same nodes (xcnf[8-12]); expt_b1 ran on slower
  xcnc nodes and expt_b4 on a mix of xcne and xcnf, so their speeds don't
  compare directly.

  Expt: expt_b0
  Change: None; the provided baseline.
  Why: To find where the time goes before changing anything.
  Result: 3.5 TPS at every client count. T3 took 4,037 ms and 68% of all time;
  T4
  633 ms, T6 399 ms, T7 66 ms. 493 serialization failures at 64 clients.
  What we learned: Rank by share of time, not weight: T3 is 5% of the mix and
  held most of the time. Single-shard updates were already fast at 1.5 ms.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Expt: expt_b1
  Change: inventory distributed on product_id and co-located with products.
  Primary key became (product_id, inventory_id). T7's two updates also filter
  on product_id.
  Why: T7 is 30% of the mix, and its four lookups by product_id scanned
  inventory
  on all 32 shards. Those scans also conflicted with every inventory write.
  Result: T7's lookups fell from 14 to 19 ms each to 1.0 to 1.6 ms. T4 fell from

  633 to 2.6 ms. Serialization failures fell from 493 to 0. TPS stayed at 3.4
  to 4.1.
  What we learned: T4 was fixed as a side effect, because its join became
  co-located. Throughput did not move because T3 was untouched and now took 82
  to 90% of the time. Node types differ a lot in speed.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Expt: expt_b2
  Change: order_items distributed on order_id and co-located with orders.
  Primary
  key became (order_id, order_item_id).
  Why: T3 joins the two tables on order_id, which was not the distribution
  column
  of order_items, so every call reshuffled all 15M order items between workers.
  Result: T3 fell from 4,037 to 10.9 ms. Throughput rose from 3.5 to 46 TPS.
  What we learned: T6 became the ceiling: 265 ms, about 90% of all time, capped
  at 4.5 per second. T3 still reaches all 32 shards.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Expt: expt_b3
  Change: Covering index orders(created_at) INCLUDE (merchant_id, total_amount),

  and a VACUUM orders after every load.
  Why: T6 scanned all 5M orders to use the 8% in its window. The index lets it
  answer from the index alone, and only VACUUM marks the pages that allow that.
  Result: T6 fell from 265 to 65 ms. Throughput rose from 46 to 230 to 250 TPS.
  Clients aborted in large numbers: 22 of 64.
  What we learned: T6 is still the ceiling at 23 to 24 per second. The workers
  are the bottleneck, not the coordinator. Citus reports a serialization
  failure at a one-worker COMMIT as a generic error, so pgbench stops that
  client.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Expt: expt_b4
  Change: BEGIN READ ONLY on T3, T4 and T6. T6 reads its time window once and
  passes it as two literal timestamps. Query plans saved after the load.
  Why: The aborts made the high-client numbers untrustworthy, and PostgreSQL
  does
  not cancel a conflict chain that starts with a read-only transaction. The
  literal window removed one suspect for T6's cost. The plans were to show
  where T6's time goes.
  Result: Client aborts fell from 41 to 2. Cancellations at PREPARE TRANSACTION
  fell from 757 to 57. About 1% of T6s are retried instead. 264 to 316 TPS from
   8 clients up.
  What we learned: The read-only flag reaches the workers. T6's plan has no
  defect; its cost is reading and grouping 410,000 rows. T3 read every order of
   the merchant on each shard (727 rows on 571 pages). T6 slows during a run
  under load, from 306 to 424 ms.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Expt: expt_b5
  Change: Index orders(merchant_id, customer_id). The same plans saved again
  after each measured run.
  Why: The expt_b4 plan showed T3's waste. The second capture was to confirm why

  T6 slows under load.
  Result: T3's shard task fell from 8.9 + 4.8 ms to 0.3 + 0.6 ms, and T3 at 1
  client from 10.9 to 6.1 ms. 110.6 TPS at 1 client and 270 to 315 TPS from 4
  clients up, against 97.9 and 215 to 249 in expt_b3.
  What we learned: T3 still takes 602 ms at 64 clients; what remains is the cost

  of reaching 32 shards on busy workers. T6's table lookups grow from 0 to
  about a third of its rows during a run, and its shard task takes about 40%
  longer. Most of those lookups trace to T5's updates, by estimate.

  Two notes on reading the table:

  - The expt_b5 gain over expt_b3 is combined. It includes expt_b4's two changes
    as well as the index, and the runs cannot split it.
  - Partitioning orders by month was added to expt_b4 and removed before it ran.
    The covering index already limits T6 to its window, and T5, T1 and T3 would
    each have had to search every partition.

❯ From now on, use simpler/less concise language (but not less technical). im
  just wondering, which transactions is blocking other transactions is it
  possible to split the T6 to a seprate read replica.

  Ran 1 shell command, wrote 2 memories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Understood on the style; I'll write in fuller sentences from here and keep the
  technical detail.

  Which transactions block the others

  Almost nothing is blocking in the locking sense. What is happening is that T6
  is using up the workers' CPUs, and every other transaction has to wait its
  turn for a CPU.

  There are two different ways one transaction can slow another down, and they
  need to be kept apart.

  Waiting for a lock. In PostgreSQL a reader never blocks a writer and a writer
  never blocks a reader. The only real lock wait is when two transactions update
  the same row. That is rare here, because the scripts pick rows at random from
  large tables. In the expt_b5 worker logs I count 18 "concurrent update"
  conflicts and no deadlocks, across about 940,000 measured transactions.
  Lock-wait logging is not switched on, so I cannot show the waits directly, but
  these counts say they are negligible.

  Waiting for a CPU. This is where the time goes. The clearest evidence is T4,
  which only reads products and inventory and so cannot be held up by any lock.
  Its SELECT takes about 0.8 ms at 1 client and 10.5 ms at 64 clients. T2's
  UPDATE slows by the same amount, to 10.1 ms. Even T4's COMMIT, which writes
  nothing, takes 9 ms. So every trip to a worker costs about 10 ms under load,
  whatever it does. That is the pattern of a queue for the CPU.

  T6 is the one filling that queue. Each T6 runs 32 shard tasks of about 17 ms
  each, which is roughly half a second of worker CPU per call. At 29 to 31 T6
  per second that comes to about 16 cores, and the four workers have exactly 16
  between them. The 17 ms comes from EXPLAIN ANALYZE, which overstates a little,
  so the true figure is somewhat lower. It is still clearly most of the
  workers' capacity.

  T3 is a smaller second offender. Its work is tiny now, but it still sends 32
  tasks into that same queue.

  Could T6 go to a separate read replica

  Citus does support this: each worker gets a streaming replica, and read
  queries are sent to the replicas. I don't think it fits our project, for three
  reasons.

  1. There is no spare hardware. The setup is fixed at five nodes with 4 CPUs
     each. A replica placed on the same nodes would run T6 on the same 16 cores,
     and would add the work of replaying every write a second time. A replica
     on extra nodes would help, but then we are no longer measuring the same
     cluster.
  2. The mix is fixed, so isolating T6 does not raise the ceiling. One
     transaction in ten is always T6, and each client must finish its T6 before
     sending anything else. Ten transactions therefore always cost about half a
     second of worker CPU for T6. That alone caps us near 16 ÷ 0.054 ≈ 300 TPS,
     which is where we are. Only making T6 cheaper, or adding CPUs, moves that.
  3. Isolation and freshness change. A PostgreSQL standby cannot run
     SERIALIZABLE transactions, and an asynchronous replica can be slightly
     behind, so T6 could miss the newest orders. Both are grey areas.

  What gets the same effect within five nodes

  The coordinator is the one machine with idle CPUs. A small, pre-aggregated
  copy of what T6 needs, kept on the coordinator, would take T6's work off the
  workers entirely. That is the materialized view or rollup idea already on the
  list, and it is the closest thing to a read replica that fits our setup.

❯ okay what shall we try next

⏺ I'd go after T6 directly next, with a table of pre-computed daily totals that
  T6 reads instead of scanning orders. Everything we have measured says T6's
  work on the workers is what sets the ceiling, so the other candidates can only
  give small gains until that changes.

  Why T6 first

  The last answer worked out that T6 uses roughly all 16 worker cores, and that
  with a fixed mix this caps us near 300 TPS. The two smaller ideas each remove
  only a slice of that:

  - Moving order_status to its own table would stop T6 getting slower during a
    run. I estimate 12 to 25% more throughput.
  - Moving orders and order_items to merchant_id would fix T3, which is 7 to 15%
    of the time.

  Neither changes the fact that each T6 reads and groups about 410,000 rows.

  The idea, and a simpler version than the proposal describes

  The proposal doc describes a rollup that T1 keeps up to date. Looking at it
  again, T1 does not need to be involved at all, because completed days never
  change. T1 only inserts orders stamped "now", and T5 only changes the status,
  which T6 does not read.

  So the design would be:

  1. A daily totals table, holding order count and revenue per merchant per day,
     for every day before the load date. It is built once at the end of each
     data load. I would make it a Citus reference table, which is cheap here
     because nothing writes to it during a run.
  2. T6 reads from two places and adds them up. For the whole days inside its
     30-day window it reads the totals table, about 30,000 small rows. For the
     parts that are not whole completed days, it reads orders through the
     existing covering index. Those parts are the partial day at the old end of
     the window, and everything from the load date onward.

  The second part still goes to all 32 shards, but each shard reads about 1,000
  rows instead of 13,500. By my estimate that brings T6 from about half a second
  of worker CPU per call down to roughly a tenth of a second. That estimate is
  untested.

  What could go wrong

  - T6 must return exactly the same rows as today. I would add a check to the
    diagnostic step in run.sh that runs the old and the new query with the same
    window, after the load and after each run, and records whether the results
    match. That makes correctness something we measure, not assume.
  - The SQL is more complex, and I have not confirmed how Citus plans a query
    that combines a reference table with a 32-shard aggregate. A short 6-minute
    job first would catch that.
  - Whether pre-computed totals are acceptable. project.md (docs/project.md)
    allows schema changes and rewritten transactions as long as each transaction
    still does what its description says, and this does. It is still a design
    choice the instructor may have a view on, so it is worth asking while the
    run is going.

  After that

  Once T6 is cheap, I cannot predict where the next limit will be; the run will
  show it. My guess at the order is T3's 32-shard reach (your merchant_id move),
  then T7's six statements.

  If you would rather take a smaller step first, the order_status table is the
  one I'd pick, since its effect is the best understood. Tell me which you want
  and I'll build it as expt_b6.

❯ explain the rollup table for expt_b6 again

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The rollup is a small table of pre-computed totals per merchant per day, built
  once at the end of each data load. T6 reads it for the old, finished days and
  reads orders only for the small, recent part of its window. Here is how that
  works and why the answer stays exact.

  The problem it solves

  Every T6 call today reads about 13,500 rows on each of the 32 shards, roughly
  410,000 rows in total, and groups them by merchant. Almost all of those rows
  are old orders whose totals never change. T6 recomputes the same sums from
  scratch about 30 times a second, and that recomputation is what fills the
  workers' CPUs.

  Why old days never change

  I checked which scripts modify orders:

  - T1 inserts new orders stamped with CURRENT_TIMESTAMP, so they always belong
    to today. Its final UPDATE sets total_amount only on the order it just
    created.
  - T5 changes only order_status, which T6 does not read.
  - Nothing deletes orders, and nothing changes created_at, merchant_id or
    total_amount on a loaded order.

  So once the load is finished, every day before the load date is frozen. A
  daily total computed at load time stays correct for the whole run, and T1
  never needs to touch the rollup.

  The table

  CREATE TABLE merchant_daily_sales(
    sales_day    date    NOT NULL,
    merchant_id  bigint  NOT NULL,
    order_count  bigint  NOT NULL,
    revenue      numeric NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (sales_day, merchant_id)
  );

  - It is filled at the end of the load with one INSERT ... SELECT ... GROUP BY
    over orders, for days before the load date. The load date's midnight is the
    "cutoff".
  - That is about 364 days × 1,000 merchants, or roughly 364,000 rows.
  - The primary key starts with sales_day, so T6 can read just the days it
    needs.
  - I'd make it a Citus reference table, which keeps a full copy on every
    worker. Normally reference tables are expensive because every write goes to
    every node, but nothing writes to this one during a run.

  How T6 uses it, with an example

  Say the load finished at 14:38 on 11 Oct and T6 runs at 15:00 the same day.
  Its window is 11 Sep 15:00 up to 11 Oct 15:00, and T6 splits it into three
  pieces:

  Piece: Partial first day
  Time range: 11 Sep 15:00 to midnight
  Read from: orders
  Rows: About none. Loaded orders on that date are stamped 14:38, before the
  window starts.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Piece: Whole days
  Time range: 12 Sep to 10 Oct, 29 days
  Read from: merchant_daily_sales
  Rows: About 29,000 rows in total
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Piece: Since the cutoff
  Time range: 11 Oct 00:00 to 15:00
  Read from: orders
  Rows: About 430 per shard from the load, plus the orders T1 placed during the
  run

  The query adds the three pieces together per merchant, then sorts and keeps
  the top 20, as T6 does today:

  SELECT merchant_id, SUM(order_count) AS order_count, SUM(revenue) AS revenue
  FROM (
    SELECT merchant_id, order_count, revenue FROM merchant_daily_sales
    WHERE sales_day >= :first_whole_day AND sales_day < :cutoff_day
    UNION ALL
    SELECT merchant_id, COUNT(*), SUM(total_amount) FROM orders
    WHERE created_at >= :window_start AND created_at < :first_whole_day
    GROUP BY merchant_id
    UNION ALL
    SELECT merchant_id, COUNT(*), SUM(total_amount) FROM orders
    WHERE created_at >= :cutoff_day AND created_at < :window_end
    GROUP BY merchant_id
  ) parts
  GROUP BY merchant_id
  ORDER BY revenue DESC
  LIMIT 20;

  The two orders pieces are written separately on purpose. Each is then a single
  range on created_at, so it can still use the covering index from expt_b3
  without visiting the table. T6's first statement, which already reads the
  clock, would also look up the cutoff date.

  Why the answer stays exact

  The three pieces cover the window with no gaps and no overlaps. The rollup
  only covers frozen days, and everything that can change during a run is read
  live from orders. That includes the newest orders, so there is no staleness,
  unlike a read replica.

  If a job runs past midnight, the "since the cutoff" piece simply grows to
  include the extra day. The answer stays correct; it just costs a little more.
  Because the data is reloaded and the rollup rebuilt before every client count,
  T6 never runs long enough after a load for the design to break.

  What it should save

  Today each T6 costs roughly 0.4 to 0.6 s of worker CPU (32 tasks of 13 to 19
  ms). With the rollup I estimate:

  - about 30 ms to read the whole days from the reference table, on one worker;
  - 64 small tasks for the two orders pieces, roughly 100 ms together.

  That is about 0.13 s per call, around a quarter of today's cost. These are
  estimates from the plans we captured, not measurements.

  A side benefit is that T6 would stop slowing down during a run. The pages T5
  dirties are almost all in old days, and T6 would no longer read those from
  orders.

  How we'd check it

  The diagnostic step in run.sh would run the old query and the new one with the
  same window, after the load and after each run, and record whether they
  agree. I would compare the full per-merchant totals, all 1,000 rows, rather
  than the top 20. Two merchants tied at 20th place could legitimately come out
  in either order, which would look like a mismatch when it isn't.

  What I'm not sure about

  - How Citus plans this query. It combines a reference table with two 32-shard
    aggregates. I expect it to compute each piece separately and add them up on
    the coordinator, but I haven't confirmed that. A short job first would show
    it.
  - Whether the instructor accepts pre-computed totals. T6 still returns exactly
    what its description asks for, so I think it is defensible, but it is worth
    asking.

  Nothing is built yet. If you want this as expt_b6, I'll set it up on top of
  expt_b5, with the correctness check included.

⏺ Write(expt_b6/transactions/T6_global_sales.sql)
  ⎿  Added 46 lines, removed 7 lines
       1  BEGIN READ ONLY;
       2
       3 +-- Split the window [window_start, window_end) into three parts
         + that cover it exactly:
       4 +--   [window_start, first_whole_day)     partial first day, rea
         +d from orders
       5 +--   [first_whole_day, cutoff_day)       whole days, read from
         +merchant_daily_sales
       6 +--   [tail_start, window_end)            everything since the c
         +utoff, read from orders
       7 +-- tail_start is cutoff_day, unless the window starts after the
         + cutoff (the rollup part is then empty).
       8  SELECT
       4 -  LOCALTIMESTAMP - (:REPORT_DAYS)::integer * INTERVAL '1 day' A
         -S window_start,
       5 -  LOCALTIMESTAMP AS window_end
       9 +  window_start,
      10 +  window_end,
      11 +  first_whole_day,
      12 +  cutoff_day,
      13 +  GREATEST(cutoff_day, first_whole_day) AS tail_start
      14 +FROM (
      15 +  SELECT
      16 +    w.window_start,
      17 +    w.window_end,
      18 +    CASE WHEN w.window_start = date_trunc('day', w.window_start
         +)
      19 +         THEN w.window_start::date
      20 +         ELSE w.window_start::date + 1
      21 +    END AS first_whole_day,
      22 +    c.cutoff_day
      23 +  FROM (
      24 +    SELECT
      25 +      LOCALTIMESTAMP - (:REPORT_DAYS)::integer * INTERVAL '1 da
         +y' AS window_start,
      26 +      LOCALTIMESTAMP AS window_end
      27 +  ) AS w
      28 +  CROSS JOIN merchant_daily_sales_cutoff AS c
      29 +) AS t
      30  \gset t6_
      31
      32  SELECT
      33    merchant_id,
      10 -  COUNT(*) AS order_count,
      11 -  SUM(total_amount) AS revenue
      12 -FROM orders
      13 -WHERE created_at >= TIMESTAMP ':t6_window_start'
      14 -  AND created_at < TIMESTAMP ':t6_window_end'
      34 +  SUM(order_count)::bigint AS order_count,
      35 +  SUM(revenue) AS revenue
      36 +FROM (
      37 +  SELECT merchant_id, order_count, revenue
      38 +  FROM merchant_daily_sales
      39 +  WHERE sales_day >= DATE ':t6_first_whole_day'
      40 +    AND sales_day < DATE ':t6_cutoff_day'
      41 +  UNION ALL
      42 +  SELECT merchant_id, COUNT(*), SUM(total_amount)
      43 +  FROM orders
      44 +  WHERE created_at >= TIMESTAMP ':t6_window_start'
      45 +    AND created_at < TIMESTAMP ':t6_first_whole_day'
      46 +  GROUP BY merchant_id
      47 +  UNION ALL
      48 +  SELECT merchant_id, COUNT(*), SUM(total_amount)
      49 +  FROM orders
      50 +  WHERE created_at >= TIMESTAMP ':t6_tail_start'
      51 +    AND created_at < TIMESTAMP ':t6_window_end'
      52 +  GROUP BY merchant_id
      53 +) AS parts
      54  GROUP BY merchant_id
      55  ORDER BY revenue DESC
      56  LIMIT 20;

⏺ expt_b6 is built and ready to submit. It is expt_b5 plus the daily sales
  rollup for T6, and nothing has been run yet.

  What changed

  Schema, in run.sh. There are two new tables.

  - merchant_daily_sales holds one row per merchant per day, with the order
    count and revenue. It is a Citus reference table, meaning every worker keeps
    a full copy. That is normally costly because every write goes to every
    node, but nothing writes to this table during a run. Its primary key is
    (sales_day, merchant_id), with the two totals included in the index, so T6
    can answer from the index alone.
  - merchant_daily_sales_cutoff holds a single row: the first day that is not in
    the rollup, which is the load date. It is a plain table on the coordinator,
    like the existing merchants table, so reading it never involves a worker.

  The loader, in run.sh. After the order totals are filled in, every load now
  does three things:

  1. It records today's date as the cutoff.
  2. It fills the rollup for every day before the cutoff, with one INSERT ...
     SELECT ... GROUP BY over orders.
  3. It runs VACUUM on the rollup, so the index-only reads work.

  Both tables are also added to the loader's TRUNCATE, so each reload starts
  clean.

  T6. The first statement reads the clock and the cutoff, and works out the
  boundaries of three ranges that together cover the 30-day window exactly:

  - the partial first day, read from orders;
  - the whole days, read from the rollup;
  - everything since the cutoff, read from orders.

  The second statement adds the three parts up per merchant, then sorts and
  keeps the top 20, as before. The two orders ranges are written as separate
  pieces so each one can still use the covering index without visiting the
  table.

  The diagnostic step. After the load and after every measured run, it now saves
  the new T6 plan and runs a correctness check. The check compares all 1,000
  per-merchant totals from the old query and the new one, and prints only_in_old
  and only_in_new. Both must be 0.

  How I checked it, and what isn't checked

  run.sh passes a syntax check under bash 5.3; the macOS bash 3.2 can't parse
  the unmodified baseline either. I also made sure the comments in the T6 script
  contain nothing pgbench would mistake for a variable.

  I have not run any of the SQL, since we don't run Citus locally. The parts I'm
  least sure of are how Citus plans T6's combined query, and the loader's
  insert from a distributed table into a reference table. If either fails, it
  fails loudly: the loader runs with set -e and stops the job, and a failing T6
  would stop the pgbench clients.

  What to do next

  Because the SQL is untested, I'd submit the short job first:

  CLIENT_COUNTS=1 DURATION=30 WARMUP_DURATION=0 sbatch run.sh

  It takes about 6 minutes. Look at two things in query-plans.log:

  - The "T6 check" line: both counts must be 0.
  - The T6 plan: it shows how Citus actually split the query.

  If both look right, run the full job.

  The design, the correctness argument and what to check are written up in
  expt_b6/README.md.

⏺ The rollup gives the right answer, but the first version of T6 had a flaw that
  made it slower at 1 client than the old query. I've fixed it in expt_b6, and
  I'd rerun the short job once before the full run.

  The good news: the answer is exact

  The check compared all 1,000 per-merchant totals between the old T6 query and
  the rollup version, and found no differences in either direction (only_in_old
  = 0, only_in_new = 0). Splitting the window and reading whole days from the
  rollup produces the same numbers as scanning orders.

  How Citus actually ran it

  The plan shows four steps:

  1. The partial first day, as 32 shard tasks. They returned 0 rows, which is
     what we expected: loaded orders on that date are stamped earlier in the day
     than the window starts.
  2. The part since the cutoff, as 32 shard tasks. Each read about 470 rows from
     the covering index in about 1 ms, close to the 430 I estimated.
  3. A single task on one worker that reads the rollup and adds the results of
     steps 1 and 2 to it. The rollup read was an index-only scan of 28,990 rows
     taking 8 ms.
  4. The final grouping and top 20, on the coordinator.

  The problem

  In step 3, the rollup part was not grouped by merchant. So that worker sent
  all 29,990 raw rows (875 kB) back to the coordinator, and the coordinator
  grouped them in step 4.

  The worker finished its own work in about 16 ms, but step 3 as a whole took
  120 ms, and step 4 took another 28 ms. The whole query came to 177 to 198 ms.
  The old T6 took 74 to 76 ms in the same kind of capture in expt_b5.

  That hurts in two ways:

  - 1-client latency goes up. At 1 client every transaction waits its turn, so a
    slower T6 lowers throughput directly.
  - It moves work onto the coordinator. At high client counts the coordinator,
    which has only 4 cores and also runs pgbench, could become the new
    bottleneck.

  The fix

  The rollup part now does its own GROUP BY merchant_id before the parts are
  combined, so step 3 returns at most 2,000 rows instead of 29,990. The result
  is unchanged, because adding up per-merchant subtotals gives the same totals
  as adding up the daily rows.

  I changed this in T6 and in the four copies of the query in the diagnostic
  step, so the check still compares like with like. run.sh still passes the bash
  5.3 syntax check.

  One caveat: the plan shows the 120 ms was spent moving and storing the rows,
  not computing, but it doesn't show which part of that was slow. I expect
  cutting the rows by about 15 times to remove most of it; the rerun will
  confirm.

  What already looks promising

  Adding up the task times in steps 1 to 3 gives roughly 60 ms of worker time
  per T6 at the start of a run. The old T6 used roughly 400 to 470 ms. That is
  the number that should raise the ceiling, since the workers were the
  bottleneck. It is an estimate from the plan; the full run will measure it.

  Next step

  Rerun the short job:

  CLIENT_COUNTS=1 DURATION=30 WARMUP_DURATION=0 sbatch run.sh

  Then check two things in query-plans.log:

  - The T6 check: it should still be 0 and 0.
  - The step 3 duration: look at the "Subplan Duration" of the subplan that
    reads merchant_daily_sales. It should fall well below 120 ms.

  If both hold, run the full job.

  Your paste was cut off after the plans, so I haven't seen the short job's
  pgbench numbers. If you sync the job folder with the rsync command from the
  top-level README, I can read them directly instead.

  I've added the short-job findings and the fix to expt_b6/README.md.

⏺ The short job (956642) failed because of a mistake I made in the last fix.
  It's corrected now, but please run the short job once more before the full
  run.

  What went wrong

  In a UNION ALL, the result takes its column names from the first part only.
  Before my fix, the first part read the rollup columns directly, so its columns
  were called order_count and revenue. My fix turned them into
  SUM(order_count)::bigint and SUM(revenue) without giving them names. The outer
  query's SUM(order_count) then had no column with that name to refer to.

  The first time pgbench picked T6, it failed with column "order_count" does not
  exist. pgbench treats that as an ordinary SQL error, not a serialization
  failure, so it stopped the client. That is why only 20 transactions completed
  and the exit code was 2. The diagnostic copies of the query failed the same
  way, so this job has no T6 plan and no correctness check.

  The fix

  The first part now names its columns: SUM(order_count)::bigint AS order_count,
  SUM(revenue) AS revenue. I made the same change in T6 and in all four
  diagnostic copies. I also re-read the rest of the query for any other problem
  of this kind, and found none. run.sh still passes the syntax check.

  What did work in this job

  The loader ran correctly:

  - The rollup has 363,836 rows. That is just under 364 days × 1,000 merchants,
    because a few merchants have no orders on some days.
  - The cutoff row was written.
  - A reload took 2 min 14 s, against 2 min 6 s in expt_b5, so building the
    rollup costs about 8 seconds per load.

  Next step

  Resubmit the short job:

  CLIENT_COUNTS=1 DURATION=30 WARMUP_DURATION=0 sbatch run.sh

  Then check three things:

  - pgbench exit code 0 in summary.csv, with a few thousand transactions instead
    of 20.
  - The T6 check in query-plans.log: both counts 0.
  - The rollup step's "Subplan Duration" in the T6 plan: well below the 120 ms
    of the first version.

  If all three hold, run the full job. I've recorded this failed job and the fix
  in expt_b6/README.md.

❯ do i have to clear the directory or should i be able to just run the job
  again for it to override the files

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You don't need to clear anything. Upload the folder again and submit; nothing
  from the earlier jobs will be overwritten or reused.

  Why nothing gets overwritten. Every output path in run.sh includes the Slurm
  job ID, so each job writes to new paths:

  - results go to slurm-results/job-<job id>/ (line 37 of run.sh);
  - the log files are citus-<job id>.out and .err;
  - the database lives in /tmp/cs14/citus-<job id> on each node (line 27), and
    that directory is deleted and recreated when the job starts.

  So the new run builds its database from scratch, and its results land in a new
  folder next to job-956534 and job-956642.

  What the upload does. Your rsync command copies files that are newer or a
  different size than the copies on the cluster. The edited run.sh and
  T6_global_sales.sql are newer, so they replace the old ones. Because the
  command has no --delete, it never removes anything on the cluster. It will
  also push your local slurm-results/job-956642 back up, which is harmless since
  that folder is already there.

  Two things to watch:

  1. Submit from inside expt_b6. run.sh finds transactions/ and the results
     folder through SLURM_SUBMIT_DIR, the directory you ran sbatch from. On the
     cluster:
  cd ~/citus_project/expt_b6
  CLIENT_COUNTS=1 DURATION=30 WARMUP_DURATION=0 sbatch run.sh
  2. Optionally, check the upload landed before submitting:
  grep -n "AS order_count, SUM(revenue) AS revenue"
  transactions/T6_global_sales.sql
     This should print line 37. If it prints nothing, the old T6 is still there.

  The two failed short-job folders can stay. They're small, and I'll read
  whichever job ID you tell me, or the newest one. If you'd rather keep
  slurm-results tidy, deleting them on both sides is safe; the README already
  records what they showed.

⏺ The short job (956776) ran cleanly and the rollup gives exactly the right
  answers, so expt_b6 is ready for the full run.

  It runs, and the answer is exact

  pgbench exited with code 0 and completed 3,411 transactions in 30 seconds,
  113.7 TPS at 1 client. No transaction failed.

  The correctness check passed both times it ran. After the load and again after
  the run, all 1,000 merchants had identical totals from the old and new T6,
  with 0 differences either way. The second check matters more, because by then
  T1 had added orders during the run. It confirms that the part read live from
  orders picks up new orders correctly.

  The fix for the shipped rows worked

  The step that reads the rollup now sends back 2,000 rows (58 kB) instead of
  29,990. That step went from 120 ms to 29 ms. The final step on the coordinator
  went from 28 ms to 3 ms.

  Why T6 is only a little faster at 1 client

  T6 averaged 52.5 ms, against 61.9 ms in expt_b5. That looks like a small gain,
  but at 1 client T6's time is now mostly coordination. Citus runs the three
  steps one after another, and two of them send a task to each of the 32 shards:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬─────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │           Step            │  Wall time in   │      Work per task       │
  │                           │    the plan     │                          │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Partial first day, 32     │ 7 to 29 ms      │ about 0.1 ms, 0 rows     │
  │ tasks                     │                 │                          │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Since the cutoff, 32      │ about 25 ms     │ about 1 ms, about 300 to │
  │ tasks                     │                 │  470 rows                │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Read and group the        │ about 29 ms     │ about 19 ms for 30,000   │
  │ rollup, 1 task            │                 │ rows                     │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Final top 20, on the      │ about 3 ms      │                          │
  │ coordinator               │                 │                          │
  └───────────────────────────┴─────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  The tasks themselves are tiny. Most of the wall time is the cost of organising
  32 tasks per step, the same thing we saw with T3.

  What should raise the ceiling

  Adding up the task times gives roughly 70 ms of worker time per T6, against
  roughly 400 to 470 ms for the old T6 at the start of a run, rising to about
  600 ms by the end. Under load, the workers' CPU was the limit, so this is the
  number that should lift throughput. It is an estimate from EXPLAIN ANALYZE,
  which slightly overstates, and only a run at higher client counts can measure
  it.

  Don't read much into the other 1-client numbers

  This run lasted 30 seconds with no warmup, and two of its five nodes (xcnf13,
  xcnf14) were not in expt_b5's set. The write transactions came out about 0.3
  to 1.9 ms slower than in expt_b5, which fits cold caches and the commit-time
  variation we have seen between runs. The full run will settle it.

  For the full run

  Submit as usual from inside expt_b6:

  sbatch run.sh

  When it's back I'll look at five things:

  - Throughput from 4 clients up, against 270 to 315 TPS in expt_b5.
  - How many T6 complete per second, against 27 to 31.
  - Whether T6 still slows down during a run.
  - The T6 check in every query-plans-after-c<clients>.log.
  - Where the new bottleneck is. If the workers are no longer saturated, it
    could be the coordinator, or T3 and T7.

  One idea for later, not for this run: steps 1 and 2 could probably be merged
  into a single 32-task step, which would cut T6's 1-client latency further.
  We'll see from the full run whether that latency matters.

  I've added this short job's findings to expt_b6/README.md.

⏺ expt_b6 worked well: throughput peaked at 539 TPS at 16 clients, against 282
  at 16 clients in expt_b5, and the rollup returned exactly the right answers
  throughout. The new limit is no longer how much work T6 does, but how many
  pieces it is split into. Results are written into expt_b6/README.md.

  Throughput

  ┌─────────┬─────────┬─────────┬────────┐
  │ Clients │ expt_b5 │ expt_b6 │ Change │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 1       │ 110.6   │ 114.4   │ +3%    │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 4       │ 270     │ 328     │ +21%   │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 8       │ 291     │ 519     │ +78%   │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 16      │ 282     │ 539     │ +91%   │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 32      │ 298     │ 508     │ +70%   │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 64      │ 315     │ 484     │ +53%   │
  └─────────┴─────────┴─────────┴────────┘

  The nodes were xcnf[10-14] this time, against xcnf[8-12] for expt_b5. They are
  the same node type with three nodes in common, so this is close to
  like-for-like but not identical.

  The rollup is exact under load

  The correctness check ran seven times: after the load and after every client
  count. Each time, all 1,000 merchants had identical totals from the old T6
  query and the rollup version. The checks after each run matter most, because
  by then T1 had added thousands of new orders inside the window. They show the
  "since the cutoff" part picks those up correctly.

  What changed for T6

  - It completes 48 to 55 times a second at 8 clients and above, up from 29
    to 31. That higher ceiling is where the extra throughput comes from.
  - It no longer slows down much during a run. At 16 clients its average went
    from 163 ms in the first two minutes to 169 ms in the last two, against 301
    to 439 ms before. At 64 clients it still rose, from 791 to 978 ms. The cause
    is that the "since the cutoff" part grows from about 450 to about 1,450
    rows per shard as T1 adds orders, and those new rows need table lookups.
  - It barely changed at 1 client (53 ms against 62). As the short job showed,
    at 1 client T6 is now mostly the cost of running its three steps one after
    another, not the work inside them.

  Why throughput falls beyond 16 clients

  T6 is still the ceiling. From 16 clients up its latency doubles each time the
  clients double, and it is still 58 to 67% of all time. But the work each T6
  does is now small, so the question is what limits it.

  The waiting still happens on the workers, not the coordinator. Statements that
  never leave the coordinator, like BEGIN, stay under 1 ms even at 64 clients.
  A statement that goes to a single worker, like T4's SELECT, takes 10 ms at 64
  clients.

  My best explanation, which I haven't measured, is the number of separate tasks
  T6 sends to the workers. Each T6 sends 65 of them: 32 for the partial first
  day, 32 for the part since the cutoff, and 1 for the rollup. At 16 clients
  that comes to about 3,500 tasks a second. By my count that is roughly half of
  all requests the workers receive, even though most of those tasks find 0 to
  1,500 rows. Each task has a fixed cost of planning, starting the executor and
  a network round trip. On top of that, sending many tasks at once makes Citus
  open extra connections to each worker, and starting a new PostgreSQL backend
  is not free. That would also explain why adding clients beyond 16 makes things
  worse instead of flat: more clients mean more of these tasks and connections
  competing for the same 16 cores.

  Smaller observations

  - T6 now takes the serialization cancellations. Its read of new orders
    conflicts with T1 inserting them, so the workers cancelled it 587 times over
    the job. pgbench retried every one, and that is under 1% of T6 calls.
  - Three clients aborted in the measured runs, all at a one-worker COMMIT, the
    same pattern as before.
  - At 16 clients, the write transactions slowed partway through the run. T7
    went from 14 to 24 ms between the third and fourth two-minute slices, while
    T6 stayed flat. The logs show no checkpoint during the run and autovacuum
    isn't logged, so I don't know the cause.

  What I'd try next

  Reduce the number of shards from 32 to 8 (citus.shard_count). With 8 shards:

  - T6 would send 17 tasks instead of 65, and T3 8 instead of 32.
  - Each worker would hold 2 shards per table instead of 8, so a T6 or T3 needs
    at most 2 connections per worker. That should cut the connection churn as
    well.
  - Transactions that go to a single shard (T1, T2, T4, T5, T7, T8, T9) would
    not be affected.

  The total rows T6 and T3 read stay the same; they are just split into fewer,
  larger pieces. Fewer shards also means less parallelism inside a single query,
  which matters little under load. Shard count is set when the tables are
  created, so it is a small change in run.sh.

  The alternative is changing T6's query so the two orders parts become a single
  32-task step. That only helps T6, so I'd try the shard count first.

  Say the word and I'll set up expt_b7.

⏺ Written for: the course graders, who know databases and Citus but haven't seen
  our work.

  Both files are ready for your review. I haven't touched docs/README.md, the
  proposal doc, or the experiment folders.

  1. README.md (new, top level)

  This is the submission README the project spec asks for, describing every
  experiment folder. The old top-level README you moved to docs/README.md is
  unchanged.

  It covers:

  - What each experiment folder contains, including a table explaining every
    output file in slurm-results/job-<id>/.
  - How a job runs: the cluster, the six client counts, the reload and restart
    before each one, warmup and measured run. It also notes that the protocol
    and settings are identical across workload B.
  - A placeholder section for workload A (expt_a0 only) for your teammates.
  - A workload B overview table: each experiment's change, job ID, nodes, mean
    TPS over the six client counts, and peak TPS. Below it is a note on which
    runs share nodes and which can't be compared directly.
  - One section per experiment, expt_b0 to expt_b6. Each lists the exact files
    and run.sh line numbers changed, the purpose, and the key result.

  While checking line numbers I found that T7's changed lines in expt_b1 are 54
  and 67, not the 53 and 65 in the old notes. The new README uses the correct
  ones.

  2. docs/report_draft.md

  This follows the structure the spec requires: cover page, Sections 1 to 6, and
  references.

  - Section 4 (Task 3, baseline) covers the setup, how the baseline design
    executes each transaction, the expt_b0 results tables, and an analysis of
    why throughput is flat at 3.5 TPS.
  - Section 5 (Task 4) covers:
    - the method (one change per step, staying out of the grey areas, plan
      capture, correctness check, the hardware caveat);
    - summary tables across all seven experiments;
    - one subsection per experiment, each with motivation, change, results table
      and analysis;
    - the final design compared with the baseline;
    - the ideas considered but not applied, with reasons.

    It points out the interesting and surprising results the spec asks for.
    Examples are expt_b1 working perfectly with no throughput gain, and the
    first rollup version being slower than the original T6.
  - Section 6 has the workload B lessons and difficulties.
  - References list the Citus and PostgreSQL documentation, the Ports and
    Grittner SSI paper, and the lecture notes.

  Every number comes from the experiment notes, and I re-checked the throughput
  figures against each job's summary.csv. Claims we didn't measure are worded as
  estimates, such as the per-task overhead explanation for expt_b6's decline
  beyond 16 clients.

  What needs your attention

  - Length. The draft is about 9,000 words, and the workload B sections alone
    would probably fill most of the 20-page limit. You asked for verbose, so I
    kept everything; it will need trimming once workload A is in.
  - Placeholders:
    - The team number is 14, taken from TEAM_ID in run.sh. Please confirm it.
    - Teammates' names and contributions are still to be filled in.
    - The AI-tools line says Claude was used for workload B; the team needs to
      complete it. The spec also requires the AI-tool files to be submitted
      separately as PDFs.
  - Figures. There are three placeholders, each noting which table holds its
    data. The spec asks for graphs, and I can generate the charts as images if
    you want.
  - References. The URLs follow the standard PostgreSQL and Citus documentation
    paths but should be checked before submission.
  - expt_b0/README.md is still inside expt_b0. The other per-experiment notes
    were moved to experiments/, and the spec lists only run.sh, transactions/,
    slurm-results/ and the two Slurm logs in each experiment folder. You may
    want to move this one too.

  │         │    TPS    │     p50     │       p95       │       p99        │
  ├─────────┼───────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ T7      │ 0.44 →    │ 1,517 → 275 │ 17,736 → 2,164  │ 26,139 → 3,465   │

  │ T4      │ 0.21 →    │ 74,221 → 53 │ 84,350 → 216 ms │ 87,024 → 381 ms  │

  ├─────────┼───────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Overall │ 3.19 →    │ 35 → 249 ms │ 133,192 →       │ 160,032 →        │

  The 64-client numbers make expt_b1 look worse overall. T4's TPS and the
  overall TPS go down, and the overall p99 jumps to 926 s. That's real, and it's
  the "the change worked but throughput didn't move" story. T3 was untouched,
  so with T7's and T4's scans gone, clients piled up in T3 (p50 of 886 s) and
  fewer of them got to run T4 or anything else. I added a bullet saying so, so
  the table doesn't read as a regression caused by the change.

  I also added two caveats from earlier analysis:

  - The 64-client run overran to about 1,005 s while clients waited on T3. TPS
    is computed over 600 s, so the true overall rate was about 1.35, not 2.26.
  - expt_b1 ran on the slower xcnc nodes, so all its figures understate the
    effect of the change.

  One correction: the column you called unsuccessful_log_records is named
  serialization_failures in summary.csv; failed_log_records holds the same 493.
  I used serialization_failures.

  I removed your two original lines on T7's per-lookup times and T4's latency
  because both were 1-client figures under an "At 64 clients" heading. If you
  want them back, they would sit better under a separate 1-client line.
