❯ does citus have partition key? what is the diff betewwn an index and
  partition key?

⏺ Yes. Citus has one, but it calls it the distribution column (also called the
  shard key). In its own metadata table pg_dist_partition the column is
  literally named partkey, which is why you'll see "partition column" in older
  docs. Plain PostgreSQL also has a separate partition key, for its own table
  partitioning. They are different features, so it's worth keeping three things
  apart.

  Citus distribution column: which shard a row lives in

  When you run create_distributed_table('orders', 'order_id'), Citus hashes each
  row's order_id to decide which of the 32 shards it goes in, and each shard
  lives on one worker. So the distribution column decides where a row physically
  lives.

  This mainly matters when a query is planned. If a query filters on the
  distribution column, Citus can compute the hash and send the query to exactly
  one shard. If it doesn't, Citus can't know where the rows are and has to send
  the query to all 32 shards. Joins work the same way: two tables distributed on
  the join column, and co-located, can be joined inside each shard. Otherwise
  rows have to be moved between workers.

  A table has exactly one distribution column. Changing it means redistributing
  the whole table.

  Index: how fast a row can be found inside a shard

  An index is a lookup structure inside each shard. When you create an index on
  a distributed table, Citus creates a copy of it on every shard. The plans
  showed names like idx_orders_merchant_customer_102104, where 102104 is the
  shard.

  An index has no effect on which shards a query is sent to. It only makes each
  shard's part of the work faster. A table can have many indexes, and adding or
  dropping one is cheap.

  Our T3 shows the difference

  orders is distributed on order_id, but T3 filters on merchant_id and
  customer_id. So:

  - The distribution column doesn't match T3's filter, and T3 always goes to all
    32 shards.
  - Before expt_b5, each of those 32 tasks was also slow. Without a suitable
    index, each shard read all of the merchant's orders and discarded most of
    them: 8.9 ms of execution plus 4.8 ms of planning.
  - The expt_b5 index fixed the second problem but not the first. Each task
    dropped to about 1 ms, but there are still 32 tasks per T3, which is why T3
    still took about 600 ms at 64 clients.
  - Changing the distribution column to merchant_id (the idea you put back on
    the list) would fix the first problem, turning T3 into a single task.

  So the two are complementary: the distribution column decides how many shards
  a query has to visit, and the index decides how much work each visit costs.

  expt_b1 got both at once. Changing inventory's distribution column to
  product_id made T7's lookups go to one shard. The new primary key (product_id,
  inventory_id) came with an index that starts with product_id, so the lookup
  inside that shard was fast too.

  Why our primary keys had to change

  Citus can only enforce uniqueness within a single shard, so it requires every
  primary key and unique constraint to include the distribution column. That is
  why inventory and order_items got compound primary keys when we redistributed
  them.

  PostgreSQL's own partition key

  PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at) splits a table into child tables on the same
  node, for example one per month. A query that filters on the partition key can
  skip partitions that can't match ("partition pruning"). A query that doesn't
  filter on it must check every partition. That is why we dropped partitioning
  orders by month in expt_b4: T5 only knows order_id, so every status update
  would have had to look in every month's partition on its shard.

  Citus can combine the two. A table can be distributed on one column across
  workers, and each shard partitioned by another, such as time. We just didn't
  find it worthwhile here.

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │             │ Citus distribution  │     Index     │     PostgreSQL      │
  │             │       column        │               │    partition key    │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │             │ which shard and     │ how fast rows │ which child table   │
  │ Decides     │ worker a row lives  │  are found    │ on the same node a  │
  │             │ on                  │ within a      │ row lives in        │
  │             │                     │ shard         │                     │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Helps a     │ filters or joins on │ filters or    │ filters on it, by   │
  │ query that  │  it, by sending it  │ sorts on its  │ skipping partitions │
  │             │ to one shard        │ columns       │                     │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ How many    │ exactly one         │ any number    │ one partitioning    │
  │ per table   │                     │               │ scheme              │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Cost to     │ redistribute the    │ create or     │                     │
  │ change      │ whole table         │ drop the      │ rebuild the table   │
  │             │                     │ index         │                     │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Must appear │                     │               │                     │
  │  in the     │ yes                 │ no            │ yes                 │
  │ primary key │                     │               │                     │
  └─────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  # CS5424 Distributed Databases — Citus Project, Team 14

  This folder contains all the benchmarking experiments our team ran for
  optimizing Citus.

  - Experiments for **workload A** (Tasks 1 and 2) are named `expt_a0`,
  `expt_a1`, and so on.
  - Experiments for **workload B** (Tasks 3 and 4) are named `expt_b0`,
  `expt_b1`, and so on.

  `expt_a0` and `expt_b0` are the provided baseline designs. Every later
  experiment is a modified iteration of the previous experiment in the same
  workload series. Each one introduces one optimization (or multiple closely
  related ones), so that the effect of the changes can be measured in
  isolation.

  ---

  ## Workload A experiments (Tasks 1 and 2)


  | Folder | Description |

  - Update `inventory` primary key from `inventory_id` to `(product_id,
  inventory_id)`.

  `160,032ms` → `926,499ms` |

  - Serialization failures (`serialization_failures`) fell from `493` to `0`.

  **Main Observations**

  - **Overall TPS did not move**, with T3 now becoming the bottleneck and
  taking up to 82% of all time at 1 client and 90% at 16.

  **Key Changes**

  | | TPS | p50 | p95 | p99 |

  **Main Observations**

  - **New bottleneck:** T6, at about 89.6% of all time, capped at about
  4.6/tps.


  ### `expt_b3` — Covering Index for T6

  **Key Changes**
  - Add a covering index `orders(created_at) INCLUDE (merchant_id,
  total_amount)`, so T6 can be answered from the index alone (index-only scan).
  - Run `VACUUM orders` at the end of every data load, so pages are marked
  all-visible and the index-only scan can skip the table.

  **Key Metrics**

  At 64 clients (`expt_b2` → `expt_b3`):

  | | TPS | p50 | p95 | p99 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

  - **Clients began to abort.** The aborts are serialization failures at a

  ### `expt_b4` — Read-only Declarations and a Literal T6 Window

  **Key Changes**
  - T3, T4 and T6 start with `BEGIN READ ONLY` instead of `BEGIN`.
  - T6 reads the current time once on the coordinator and passes the window to
  its query as two literal timestamps, instead of each worker evaluating
  `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP`.
  - Diagnostic only: `run.sh` saves the query plans of T6, T3 and T5 to
  `query-plans.log` after the first data load.

  **Key Metrics**

  At 64 clients (`expt_b3` → `expt_b4`):

  | | TPS | p50 | p95 | p99 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

  → `610ms` |
  | T6 | `24.48` → `31.17` | `1,213ms` → `1,242ms` | `1,725ms` → `1,758ms` |
  `2,038ms` → `2,075ms` |
  | Overall | `249.24` → `316.00` | `35ms` → `37ms` | `1,231ms` → `1,242ms` |
  `1,619ms` → `1,656ms` |

  **Main Observations**

  - **The aborts are fixed.** PostgreSQL does not cancel a conflict chain that
  starts with a read-only transaction, so the remaining cancellations moved to
  T6's read, which pgbench retries (about 1% of T6s).
  - **T6's plan is clean:** an index-only scan with 0 table lookups. Its
  remaining cost is the work itself, about 410,000 rows read and grouped per
  call.
  - **T3 is wasteful:** on every shard it reads all of the merchant's orders,
  then discards all but the customer's.
  - **T6 slows down during a run under load**, from 306ms to 424ms average at
  16 clients.


  ### `expt_b5` — Index for T3

  **Key Changes**
  - Add an index on `orders(merchant_id, customer_id)`, so each T3 shard task
  goes straight to the customer's orders.
  - Diagnostic only: `run.sh` also saves the query plans after every measured
  run, to `query-plans-after-c<N>.log`.

  **Key Metrics**

  At 64 clients (`expt_b4` → `expt_b5`):

  | | TPS | p50 | p95 | p99 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | T3 | `15.83` → `15.80` | `707ms` → `620ms` | `990ms` → `924ms` | `1,244ms`
  → `1,111ms` |
  | T6 | `31.17` → `31.05` | `1,242ms` → `1,266ms` | `1,758ms` → `1,757ms` |
  `2,075ms` → `2,022ms` |
  | Overall | `316.00` → `315.12` | `37ms` → `41ms` | `1,242ms` → `1,263ms` |
  `1,656ms` → `1,650ms` |

  - `expt_b4` ran on mixed nodes; `expt_b5` ran on the same nodes as `expt_b3`.
  Against `expt_b3`, overall TPS at 64 clients rose from `249.24` to `315.12`.
  - At 1 client, T3 fell from `10.9ms` (`expt_b3`, same nodes) to `6.1ms`
  average.

  **Main Observations**

  - **T3's work per shard is nearly gone** (`8.9ms` execution + `4.8ms`
  planning → `0.3ms` + `0.6ms`), yet T3 still takes about `600ms` at 64
  clients. What remains is the cost of reaching 32 shards on busy workers.
  - **The cause of T6's slowdown is confirmed.** The share of T6's index
  entries that need a table lookup grows from 0 after the load to about a third
  after a loaded run, mostly because T5's updates clear the pages' all-visible
  marks.
  - **T6 is still the bottleneck**, at 63.4% of all time, capped at about 31
  T6/s.


  ### `expt_b6` — Daily Sales Rollup for T6

  **Key Changes**
  - New reference table `merchant_daily_sales` holding order count and revenue
  per merchant per day, and a one-row coordinator table
  `merchant_daily_sales_cutoff` recording the first day not in it (the load
  date).
  - At the end of every data load, `run.sh` fills the rollup for every day
  before the cutoff and runs `VACUUM` on it.
  - T6 splits its 30-day window into three exact parts: whole days are read
  from the rollup; the partial first day and everything since the cutoff are
  read from `orders`.
  - Diagnostic only: after the load and after every measured run, `run.sh`
  checks that the new T6 returns the same per-merchant totals as the original
  T6 query.

  **Key Metrics**

  At 64 clients (`expt_b5` → `expt_b6`):

  | | TPS | p50 | p95 | p99 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | T6 | `31.05` → `47.90` | `1,266ms` → `870ms` | `1,757ms` → `1,077ms` |
  `2,022ms` → `1,174ms` |
  | T3 | `15.80` → `24.17` | `620ms` → `161ms` | `924ms` → `226ms` | `1,111ms`
  → `261ms` |
  | Overall | `315.12` → `483.53` | `41ms` → `39ms` | `1,263ms` → `869ms` |
  `1,650ms` → `1,031ms` |

  - Peak throughput rose from `315.1` TPS (64 clients) to `539.4` TPS (16
  clients). At 16 clients alone, it went from `281.9` to `539.4`.
  - The correctness check found `0` differences in all 7 captures.
  - `expt_b6` ran on `xcnf[10-14]`: the same node type as `expt_b5`, sharing
  three of its five nodes.

  **Main Observations**

  - **T6 reads far less.** Days before the load date never change during a run,
  so their totals are computed once per load. T6 now reads about 29,000 rollup
  rows plus about 450 to 1,500 recent orders per shard, instead of about
  13,500 orders per shard.
  - **T6 no longer slows down during a run** at 16 clients (`163ms` → `169ms`,
  against `301ms` → `439ms` in `expt_b5`).
  - **T6 is still the largest share of time** (66.7% at 64 clients), now capped
  at about 48 to 55 T6/s.
  - **Throughput falls beyond 16 clients**, from `539.4` to `483.5` TPS at 64.
  The likely cause, not measured, is the fixed cost of T6's 65 small shard
  tasks per call and the worker connections they open.


  This may or may not be related to the current task.</ide_selection>

⏺ Yes. The spec asks only that the README "provides a description for each of
  the experiment sub-folders". Your format does that for workload B and goes
  further: each experiment says what changed, shows the effect, and says what it
  led to next. A grader can match each folder to its section without opening
  the files.

     (3.19 → 2.26) and the overall p99 rising to 926 s. Without context, that
     reads as the change making things worse. Two facts explain it, and both
     were cut:

     - Its 64-client run overran to about 1,005 s, so the true rate was about
       1.35 TPS.

     the baseline section has none. Something like "about 3.5 TPS at every
     client count; T3 takes about two thirds of all time" would give the reader
     the starting point.
  3. Job IDs and nodes. You removed the overview table, which is fine since each
     folder has only one job-<id> folder. But it was the only place listing
     which nodes each run used, and that matters for which comparisons are fair.
     A short "Nodes" line per experiment, or a small table at the top, would
     restore it.

  required layout:

    experiments in the same format, so the README stays consistent.

    You moved the other notes to experiments/, so this one probably belongs
    there too. Likewise, docs/ and experiments/ probably shouldn't go into
    citus_project_team_14/ when you zip it.

❯ An ordinary index, or a BRIN index, on created_at would not help much.
  Because the 365 load timestamps are spread evenly through the table, nearly
  every table page holds some rows inside the window, so fetching those rows
  through an index still touches almost every page. A covering index that also
  holds merchant_id and total_amount lets PostgreSQL answer T6 from the index
  alone, with an index-only scan, reading only the 8% of index entries inside
  the window.
  ---
  explain in simple terms why this is the case?

⏺ The short version: a table is read a page at a time, not a row at a time. If
  the rows you want are scattered across almost every page, you end up reading
  almost every page whichever way you find them. A covering index avoids the
  table entirely.

  How the table is stored

  PostgreSQL stores a table in 8 KB pages, each holding on the order of a
  hundred orders rows. When it needs a row, it reads the whole page that row is
  on. So the cost of a query is mostly the number of pages it reads, not the
  number of rows it wants.

  Why every page has rows from the window

  The loader gives order number i this date:

  created_at = load time − ((i × 31) mod 365) days

  Consecutive orders therefore jump around the year: order 1 is 31 days old,
  order 2 is 62 days old, order 3 is 93 days old, and so on. Rows are written to
  pages in roughly that order, so every page ends up holding a mix of dates
  from across the whole year.

  T6 wants the last 30 days, which is about 8% of orders. If a page holds about
  100 rows and each has an 8% chance of being in the window, a typical page
  contains about 8 wanted rows. The chance that a page contains none at all is
  about 0.92^100 ≈ 0.02%. So practically every page has something T6 needs.

  Why an ordinary index doesn't help

  An ordinary index on created_at is like a catalogue sorted by date. It can
  quickly list all the rows in the last 30 days. But it stores only the date and
  the row's location, not merchant_id or total_amount. To get those, PostgreSQL
  still has to go to the table and read the page each row is on. Since nearly
  every page has a wanted row, that means reading nearly the whole table, just
  in a jumbled order. That is no better than simply scanning the table.

  Why a BRIN index doesn't help

  A BRIN index is a much smaller summary. For each block of pages, say 128 at a
  time, it stores only the oldest and newest created_at in that block. A query
  can skip a block whose range doesn't overlap the window.

  Here every block contains dates from the whole year. So every block's range
  runs from about a year ago to today, every block "might match", and nothing is
  skipped. BRIN only works when the data is physically sorted by the column,
  such as a log table that is only ever appended to.

  Why a covering index works

  The covering index orders(created_at) INCLUDE (merchant_id, total_amount) is
  sorted by date, and each entry carries the other two columns T6 needs. That
  changes two things:

  1. The window is one contiguous slice of the index. Because the entries are
     sorted by date, all of the last 30 days sit next to each other, so
     PostgreSQL reads only that slice: about 8% of the entries.
  2. It never has to visit the table. Every column T6 uses is already in the
     index entry. This is called an index-only scan.

  To use the library picture: the books are shelved in random order, and you
  need the author and price of every book from last month. With an ordinary
  catalogue you'd still walk to almost every shelf. A covering index is a
  catalogue whose cards already list the author and price, so you never leave
  the catalogue drawer.

  That's what the plans showed. Each shard read about 90 index pages to get its
  roughly 12,800 entries in the window, instead of scanning its whole share of
  the table.

  There is one condition: the table pages must be marked all-visible, or
  PostgreSQL still visits the table to check that each row is visible to the
  query. That is why expt_b3 added VACUUM orders after the load, and why T6
  slowed down during runs as T5's updates cleared those marks.

⏺ Here is the same table with 64 clients in place of 16, computed from expt_b0's
  transaction_metrics.csv. Share of time is each transaction's count multiplied
  by its average latency, as a fraction of the total. The 1-client columns are
  unchanged.

  ┌────────┬────────────────┬─────────────┬────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │        │ Avg latency, 1 │  Share of   │ Avg latency,   │   Share of    │
  │  Txn   │   client (ms)  │  time, 1    │  64 clients    │   time, 64    │
  │        │                │   client    │      (ms)      │    clients    │
  ├────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ T3     │ 4,037          │ 67.6%       │ 145,216        │ 58.8%         │
  ├────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ T6     │ 399            │ 15.1%       │ 7,664          │ 5.7%          │
  ├────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ T4     │ 633            │ 9.5%        │ 69,987         │ 30.3%         │
  ├────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ T7     │ 66             │ 7.2%        │ 5,655          │ 5.1%          │
  ├────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ T1     │ 11             │ 0.4%        │ 165            │ 0.1%          │
  ├────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ T2,    │ 1.4 to 1.6     │             │                │ under 0.1%    │
  │ T5,    │ each           │ 0.1% each   │ 15 to 33 each  │ each          │
  │ T8, T9 │                │             │                │               │
  └────────┴────────────────┴─────────────┴────────────────┴───────────────┘

  At 64 clients, T4's share doubles to 30% (from 15.5% at 16 clients), while T6
  and T7 drop to about 5% each. That fits the repartition joins being the worst
  part of the baseline under load: T3 and T4, the two transactions that
  reshuffle rows between workers, take 89% of all time between them.

  I haven't edited the report, so you can paste the table where you want it. If
  you swap it in for the 16-client version, the paragraph after it in Section
  4.4 quotes the 16-client figure (48.8 s for T3). That would need changing to
  145 s.

⏺ That failed because zsh doesn't split a variable holding several file names
  into separate arguments. Retrying with the files listed explicitly:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The two numbers come from the PostgreSQL logs of the four worker nodes in each
  experiment's results folder, not from pgbench's CSV files. I've just
  recounted them and they reproduce: 14 for expt_b3 and 328 for expt_b4.

  Where they are. Each job folder has one log per node, and topology.txt says
  which nodes were workers:

  - expt_b3 (job 929009): xcnf9, xcnf10, xcnf11, xcnf12 →
    expt_b3/slurm-results/job-929009/xcnf{9,10,11,12}-postgresql.log
  - expt_b4 (job 931181): xcne7, xcnf0, xcnf1, xcnf2 →
    expt_b4/slurm-results/job-931181/{xcne7,xcnf0,xcnf1,xcnf2}-postgresql.log

  What is counted. Whenever a worker cancels a transaction for a serialization
  conflict, it logs four lines, like this one from expt_b4:

  ERROR:  could not serialize access due to read/write dependencies among
  transactions
  DETAIL:  Reason code: Canceled on conflict out to pivot 59922, during read.
  HINT:  The transaction might succeed if retried.
  STATEMENT:  SELECT merchant_id, count(*) AS order_count, sum(total_amount) AS
  revenue
              FROM public.orders_102105 orders WHERE ((created_at ...

  The STATEMENT line shows which query was cancelled. T6's shard query is the
  only one that reads orders with WHERE ((created_at, so counting the
  cancellations whose statement matches that gives T6's count:

  cat expt_b4/slurm-results/job-931181/xcne7-postgresql.log \
      expt_b4/slurm-results/job-931181/xcnf[0-2]-postgresql.log \
    | grep -A3 'could not serialize' | grep 'STATEMENT:' \
    | grep -c 'orders WHERE ((created_at'

  For expt_b3, use the xcnf9 and xcnf1[0-2] logs in job-929009 instead.

  Two caveats for the report wording:

  - These are whole-job counts. They cover all six client counts, warmups
    included, since each server log spans the entire job. They are not the
    measured runs only.
  - They count cancelled shard tasks, not T6 transactions. One T6 call sends 32
    shard tasks, and a cancellation is logged for whichever task was cancelled.
    Normally one per cancelled call, but this isn't guaranteed. The pgbench
    figure, how many times pgbench retried T6, is a different number. It is in
    each result-c<N>.txt, for example 216 retries at 64 clients in expt_b4.

  So "T6 was cancelled 328 times over the whole job, against 14 before" is
  accurate. "Transactions cancelled of T6 rose to 328" slightly overstates
  what's counted. "T6's shard queries were cancelled 328 times" is the precise
  version.

⏺ That's close. It cites the source, which is good, but it has the same two
  small problems as before.

  1. The count is of cancelled shard queries, not T6 transactions. It's usually
     one per cancelled T6, but not guaranteed, so "transactions cancelled"
     claims slightly more than was counted.
  2. It covers the whole job, all client counts and warmups included. Without
     saying so, a reader may assume it's the measured runs, or one particular
     client count.

  The wording "Transactions cancelled of T6" is also a bit awkward, and "xx" can
  be named more precisely. Something like:

  ▎ Cancellations of T6's shard queries rose from 14 to 328 over the whole job
  ▎ (counted from the four workers' x<node>-postgresql.log files).

  Or, if you want to keep it closer to your phrasing:

  ▎ T6's queries were cancelled 328 times, against 14 before, across the whole
  ▎ job (counted from the workers' x<node>-postgresql.log files).

  If space allows, you could add pgbench's own figure next to it. That shows
  these cancellations were absorbed rather than failing transactions: pgbench
  retried T6 216 times at 64 clients, about 1% of T6 calls, and no transaction
  failed.

  # CS5242 Final Report

  # 4\. Task 3: Baseline design (Workload B)

  Workload B is made up of the same 9 transactions but with a different weight
  mix. Most notably, 80% of the transactions involve writing data (T1, T2, T5,
  T7, T8, T9). T7 itself occupies the largest proportion at 30%, 3 times higher
  than the next most frequent transaction.

  | Txn | Name | Weight | Description |
  | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
  | T1 | Place Order | 10% | Place an pending order for the customer, decrement
  the stock count, and insert the order items. Rollback if customer or product
  is missing, or there isn’t sufficient stock. |
  | T2 | Update Inventory | 10% | Adds a random delta to a single product’s
  stock. Skip if the stock count becomes \< 0\. |
  | T3 | Customer Order History | 5% | Retrieve 20 most recent orders and the
  order items. |
  | T4 | Product Lookup | 5% | Retrieve information on a single product and its
  inventory quantity. |
  | T5 | Update Order Status | 10% | Update a random order’s status. |
  | T6 | Global Sales | 10% | Aggregate sales metrics for all merchants and get
  the top 20\. |
  | T7 | Inventory Transfer | 30% | Moves stock between two random products. |
  | T8 | Update Customer | 10% | Updates a single customer’s email |
  | T9 | Update Product | 10% | Updates the price of a single product |

  *Table 4.1 Transaction types and weights for workload B*

  ## Experiment setup

  Every benchmark experiment runs as a single Slurm job on NUS SoC compute
  cluster. The job deploys a 5-node Citus PostgreSQL distributed database, with
  a single coordinator and 4 workers. `pgbench` runs on the coordinator node
  and connects only to the coordinator, such that every statement passes
  through it.

  * The software versions are Citus `14.1.0` on PostgreSQL `18.6`.

  The default settings used for the benchmarking are:

  * `shared_buffers` 1 GB
  * `work_mem` 32 MB
  * `max_connections` 1000,
  * `SERIALIZABLE` isolation
  * repartition joins enabled
  * `citus.max_adaptive_executor_pool_size` 4
  * `citus.max_shared_pool_size` 300

  ## `expt_b0`

  | Clients | TPS | p50 (ms) | p95 (ms) | p99 (ms) | Txn  retried |
  Serialization failures | pgbench Exit Code (result-cX.txt) |
  | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
  | 1 | 3.77 | 14 | 666 | 4,013 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
  | 4 | 3.46 | 44 | 12,917 | 17,040 | 77 | 10 | 0 |
  | 8 | 3.64 | 59 | 6,736
  ──── (167 lines hidden) ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ce the amount of data read by analytics queries such as T6 is pre-aggregating
  and summarising raw tables into rollup tables. In our case, the order counts
  and revenue per day before the load date can be computed once and reused by
  every T6.

  ### Key Changes

  1. A new Citus Reference table, `merchant_daily_sales(sales_day, merchant_id,
  order_count, revenue)`, with primary key `(sales_day, merchant_id)` and the
  two totals included in the index so it can be read by index-only scans.
  Reference tables are normally expensive to write, because every write goes to
  every node, but for this benchmarking experiment, the tradeoff could be
  worth it.
  2. `merchant_daily_sales_cutoff(cutoff_day)`: a one-row plain table on the
  coordinator that records the first day not in the rollup.
  3. At the end of every load, `run.sh` records the cutoff, fills the rollup
  table with one `INSERT ... SELECT ... GROUP BY` over `orders`, and runs
  `VACUUM` on it.
  4.

  ### Benchmark Results and Analysis

  |  | TPS | p50 Latency (ms) | p95 Latency (ms) | p99 Latency (ms) |
  | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
  | T6 | 31.05 → 47.90 | 1,266 → 870 | 1,757 → 1,077 | 2,022 → 1,174 |
  | T3 | 15.80 → 24.17 | 620 → 161 | 924 → 226 | 1,111 → 261 |
  | Overall | 315.12 → 483.53 | 41 → 39 | 1,263 → 869 | 1,650 → 1,031 |

  *Table 5.6: Comparing `expt_b6` against `expt_b5` on T6 at 64 clients*

  ## Because this rewrite could silently change T6's answer, we added
  additional verification. After the load and after every measured run,we run
  the original T6 query and the new one with the same window and compare all
  1,000 per-merchant totals in both directions with `EXCEPT`. We compared the
  full totals rather than the top 20, because merchants tied for 20th place
  could legitimately appear in either order. The validation check found 0
  differences in all captures.

  | Clients | Overall TPS | T6 avg latency (ms) | T6 completed per second | T6
  share of all time |
  | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
  | 1 | 114.4 | 53 | 11.3 | 60.2% |
  | 4 | 327.5 | 61 | 33.1 | 50.1% |
  | 8 | 518.9 | 85 | 51.8 | 54.8% |
  | 16 | 539.4 | 170 | 54.6 | 57.9% |
  | 32 | 507.7 | 421 | 50.6 | 67.6% |
  | 64 | 483.5 | 872 | 47.9 | 66.7% |

  Reframing T6 into querying the rollup tables increases throughput by \~50%.
  However, T6 is still the ceiling: from 16 clients up its latency doubles each
  time the clients double, and it is still 58 to 67% of all time. But its work
  is now small.

  1. Data errors

  Table 5.5 (line 204) has the wrong experiment's numbers. It is captioned
  expt_b5 against expt_b4, but its values (T3 15.80 → 24.17, Overall 315.12 →
  483.53) are expt_b5 → expt_b6. Those same rows appear again in Table 5.6. The

  ┌─────────┬─────────────────┬───────────┬───────────────┬───────────────┐

  With the correct numbers, expt_b5 looks flat at 64 clients. The text then
  needs to explain why: expt_b4 ran on mixed nodes, and T3 at 64 clients is
  dominated by waiting on busy workers. It also needs the evidence that the
  index worked:

  - per-shard work fell from 8.9 ms execution + 4.8 ms planning to 0.3 + 0.6 ms;
  - T3 at 1 client fell from 10.9 to 6.1 ms, against expt_b3 on the same nodes;
  - overall TPS at 64 clients went from 249 to 315, also against expt_b3.

  "The new index reduced T3's per-shard by ~5x" (line 206) matches no
  measurement. Per-shard time fell about 15 times (13.7 → 0.9 ms), and execution
  alone about 30 times. A word is also missing: "per-shard work".

  Table 4.2's exit-code column (line 39) is wrong at 8 and 16 clients. It shows
  0 everywhere, but summary.csv has exit code 2 at 8 and 16 clients, with one
  client aborted in each run. The exit code comes from summary.csv
  (pgbench_exit_code), not result-cX.txt. result-cX.txt is where the abort
  messages are.

  2. Explanations that are wrong

  The cause of the aborts in expt_b4 is backwards (line 153). The draft says
  two-phase commit cancellations raise an error that makes pgbench abort. The
  expt_b3 server logs showed the opposite:

  - The 757 cancellations at PREPARE TRANSACTION, the two-phase case, reached
    pgbench as ordinary serialization failures and were retried.
  - The aborts came from the 51 cancellations at a plain COMMIT, when all of a
    transaction's writes landed on one worker. Citus reports that as could not
    commit transaction for shard ... on at least one active node, which pgbench
    doesn't recognise as retryable, so the client stops.

  Figure 5.4.1 shows the retried kind of error. It is from expt_b3's xcnf9 log
  and shows a cancellation at PREPARE TRANSACTION. The snippet that matches the
  explanation is the coordinator's could not commit transaction for shard ...,
  with the worker's cancellation at STATEMENT: COMMIT.

  Two smaller points in the same paragraph:

  - SSI detects a "dangerous structure" (two consecutive read-write
    dependencies), not necessarily a full cycle.
  - READ ONLY doesn't reduce conflicts. It stops PostgreSQL cancelling a
    conflict chain that starts with a read-only transaction.

  "Perform VACUUM to update the index" (line 137) is wrong. VACUUM marks table
  pages all-visible in the visibility map, which is what lets the index-only
  scan skip the table. It doesn't change the index. It also runs at the end of
  every data load, which is worth saying.

  3. Statements that don't match the tables, or need caveats

  The method versus expt_b1 (lines 61 and 65). Section 5 says each optimization
  targeted "whichever transaction had the largest share of time", and Section 4
  ends the same way. But expt_b1 targeted T7 and T4 while T3 held 59 to 68% of
  the time; T7 had only 5 to 7%. Either present expt_b1 as the first hypothesis
  and the lesson it taught (its flat throughput is your "surprising result"), or
  say that the share-of-time rule was adopted after expt_b1.

  "The top three transactions ... are T3, T4 and T7" (line 61) doesn't match
  Table 4.3. That was true at 16 clients, the table you replaced. The table you
  have now gives T3, T6 and T4 at 1 client, and T3, T4 and T6 at 64.

  "T3 ... occupies more than 60%" (line 61) holds at 1 client (67.6%) but not at
  64 (58.8%). "About 59 to 68%" covers both.

  "Throughput ... remains unmoved and dropped slightly" (line 89) needs two
  caveats that are missing.

  - expt_b1 ran on slower xcnc nodes.
  - Its 64-client run overran to about 1,005 s, so its true rate was about 1.35
    TPS, not 2.26.

  At 64 clients that is a large drop, not a slight one, and at the other client
  counts it is flat or slightly up (for example 3.54 → 4.10 at 16). Without the
  caveats, Table 5.1 reads as the change causing a regression.

  "Overall throughput rose about 20 times" (line 110) is inflated by expt_b1's
  overstated, slow-node figure. Against expt_b0, on the same nodes, it was 3.19
  → 46.04 at 64 clients, about 14 times. On the mean over all client counts it
  was about 12 times.

  "T3 has accounted for almost all of the time" (line 93): it was 59 to 68% in
  expt_b0 and 82 to 90% in expt_b1. "Most of the time" is more accurate.

  expt_b4's TPS comparison (Table 5.4) needs the caveat that expt_b4 ran on
  mixed xcne/xcnf nodes. The abort counts compare fine; TPS and latency don't.

  expt_b5: "65.7% of all time, about 29 per second" (line 206) are 16-client
  figures, while every table in Section 5 is at 64 clients (63.4%, about 31 per
  second). Say which client count you mean.

  expt_b6: "increases throughput by ~50%" (line 240) is +53% at 64 clients, but
  +91% at 16 clients and +59% on the mean. The peak of 539 TPS at 16 clients
  isn't mentioned, although it's the headline result.

  expt_b6: "58 to 67% of all time" should be 58 to 68%, since it is 67.6% at 32
  clients.

  expt_b6: the paragraph ends with "But its work is now small." The table
  directly above shows throughput falling from 539 to 484 TPS beyond 16 clients,
  and the text never addresses it. The likely cause is the per-task and
  connection overhead of T6's 65 shard tasks. That is unmeasured and should be
  worded as a hypothesis.

  4. Missing content the logic depends on

  expt_b6 key change 4 is empty (line 217). That is where the core change
  belongs: T6 splitting its window into the partial first day and the part since
  the cutoff, both read from orders, plus whole days read from the rollup.

  Why the rollup stays correct is never stated. The reasons are:

  - T1 only inserts orders stamped now;
  - T5 changes only order_status;
  - nothing deletes orders;
  - the data is reloaded before every client count.

  Without this, a reader would assume the rollup goes stale during a run. The
  correctness check is mentioned, but not why the design is exact.

  The reference table's justification (line 214) is vague. "The tradeoff could
  be worth it" should be the actual reason: nothing writes to the table during a
  run, so its write cost is never paid while benchmarking.

  Experiment setup doesn't give the benchmark protocol. Later sections depend on
  it: client counts, 60 s warmup plus 600 s measured, reload and restart before
  each count, up to 3 tries, and that pgbench retries only serialization and
  deadlock failures and stops a client on any other error. Without that last
  point, exit code 2 and "retry rather than abort" can't be understood.

  expt_b3: "pgbench aborted with exit_code=2" — pgbench didn't abort. Individual
  clients did: 1, 3, 5, 10 and 22 at 4 to 64 clients, which is how you get 41
  in expt_b4. Giving those counts here would set up expt_b4.

  The expt_b3 formula (line 131) names the column created_time instead of
  created_at, and has no unit. It should read: created_at = load time −
  ((order_id × 31) mod 365) days.

  Figure 5.4.2 is from the plain EXPLAIN (VERBOSE) plan, so it doesn't contain
  the Heap Fetches: 0 line the text cites. The EXPLAIN ANALYZE snippet from the
  same log has it.

  Node caveats are absent throughout. expt_b1 ran on xcnc, expt_b4 on mixed
  nodes, and expt_b6 on xcnf[10-14] against expt_b5's xcnf[8-12]. These caveats
  are what make the expt_b1, expt_b2 and expt_b4 comparisons defensible.

  opportunities for optimization") but doesn't explain them. The core insight of
  Task 3 is three things:

  - pgbench is closed-loop, so throughput is flat because the workers are
    already saturated;

  - T4's repartition join, T6's full scan and T7's four 32-shard lookups make up

  - expt_b5: the T3 index removed 94% of each shard's work, yet T3 was barely

  - expt_b6: the first version of the rollup was slower than the original T6,
    because it shipped 30,000 rows to the coordinator.
  - Within-run slowdown: T6 got slower during each run as T5's updates cleared
    the visibility map.
  - Beyond 16 clients: after expt_b6, adding clients reduced throughput.

❯ im curious, from expt_b5 onwards, we take the plan after each client
  increases, when waht is the plan file without the client count meausring and
  captured?

⏺ query-plans.log is captured once, right after the initial data load and before
  any benchmark runs. It shows the plans on freshly loaded, freshly vacuumed
  data that no transaction has touched yet.

  The order inside run.sh is:

  1. Initial data load. generate_data loads everything, fills in the order
     totals, runs VACUUM orders, then ANALYZE (from expt_b6, it also builds and
     vacuums the rollup).
  2. The capture. capture_plans runs once and writes query-plans.log. No pgbench
     client has run yet.
  3. The benchmark loop. Before each client count, including the first, the
     servers restart and the data is truncated and reloaded. You can see this in
     benchmark.log: "Resetting database ... before client-count c1". Then come
     the 60 s warmup and the 600 s measured run.
  4. After each measured run (from expt_b5), capture_plans runs again and writes
     query-plans-after-c<N>.log, before the reload for the next client count.

  So the two kinds of file bracket every run:

  - query-plans.log is the "before" state. It technically comes from its own
    load, but every client count starts from an identical reload, so it is
    effectively the starting state of every run. It is why T6 showed Heap
    Fetches: 0 there: the VACUUM had just marked every page all-visible.
  - query-plans-after-c<N>.log is the "after" state for N clients, on the data
    as 60 s of warmup plus 600 s of measured run left it. That is where the
    table lookups had grown to about a third of T6's rows.

  Neither capture falls inside a measured window, so neither affects the
  benchmark numbers.

  Still to fix

  1. The expt_b4 explanation (line 153) still says the opposite of what the logs
  show, and its last sentence is cut off. It still implies that the two-phase
  commit path makes pgbench abort, but the figure below it now correctly shows
  the other path. What the logs show is:

  - cancellations at PREPARE TRANSACTION (the two-phase case) are reported as
    serialization failures, and pgbench retries them;
  - the aborts come from a plain COMMIT on one worker, which Citus reports as
    could not commit transaction for shard ..., and pgbench doesn't retry.

  The paragraph also ends mid-sentence ("helps by preventing Postgres from
  cancelling"), and "a two consecutive read-write dependency" should be "two
  consecutive read-write dependencies". A possible rewrite:

  ▎ Under SERIALIZABLE, PostgreSQL cancels a transaction when it detects two
  ▎ consecutive read-write dependencies among concurrent transactions. When the
  ▎ cancellation hits a multi-worker transaction at PREPARE TRANSACTION, pgbench
  ▎ sees an ordinary serialization failure and retries it. When it hits a
  ▎ transaction whose writes all landed on one worker, at a plain COMMIT, Citus
  ▎ reports it as could not commit transaction for shard ..., which pgbench does
  ▎ not recognise as retryable, so the client aborts (Figure 5.4.1). PostgreSQL
  ▎ does not cancel a dependency chain that starts with a read-only
  ▎ transaction, so declaring the read-only transactions as READ ONLY should
  ▎ remove most of these cancellations.

  2. "The top three transactions ... are T3, T4 and T7" (line 61) still doesn't
  match Table 4.2.2. That was the 16-client ranking. Your table gives T3, T6 and
  T4 at 1 client, and T3, T4 and T6 at 64.

  3. The verification paragraph (line 234) is still a ## heading. It will render
  as a large title. Remove the ## .

  Smaller points

  - "58 to 67% of all time" (line 247) should be 58 to 68%, since it is 67.6% at
    32 clients.
  - "Dropped slightly" (line 89) understates it. At 64 clients the table shows a
    29% drop. "Did not improve, and fell at 64 clients" fits the table better.
  - The expt_b6 peak is never mentioned. The best result of the whole series is
    539 TPS at 16 clients, against 282 at 16 in expt_b5, but the text only gives
    the 64-client +50%. Removing the TPS column from Table 5.6.2 also removed
    the evidence for it, so one sentence would bring it back.
  - The link from expt_b5 to expt_b6 is now missing. The sentence saying T6 was
    again the largest share (about 63 to 66%) with a clean plan, so the only way
    forward was to read fewer rows, was removed. Without it, expt_b6 starts
    without a reason.
  - Why the rollup stays correct (line 215) is still unstated. The reason is
    that T1 only inserts orders stamped now, T5 only changes the status, and
    nothing deletes orders. The verification paragraph shows it works, but not
    why the design is exact. One sentence would do it.
  - The expt_b5 result could use one sentence of explanation. Per-shard work
    fell about 15 times but throughput didn't move. That's because T3 still
    reaches 32 shards on busy workers, and T6 still dominates.
  - The experiment setup still lacks the benchmark protocol. That means the
    client counts, the 60 s warmup plus 600 s measured run, the reload before
    each count, and up to 3 tries. Most important is that pgbench retries only
    serialization and deadlock failures and stops a client on any other error.
    Sections 5.3 and 5.4 depend on that last point.
  - Nits:
    - line 11: T3's LIMIT 20 counts order-item rows, not orders;
    - line 132: "all the pages" should be "almost all the pages";
    - line 149: "incomplete" is odd; the results exist, just measured with fewer
      live clients;
    - Table 5.6.1's caption says "on T6", but it also has a T3 row.

  The bigger suggestions from before are still open but aren't errors: one
  throughput graph and an overall summary table across expt_b0 to expt_b6. Once
  items 1 to 3 are fixed, I'd consider the data and the logic of the B sections
  sound.

❯ how do i generate a link to this conversation?

⏺ As far as I know, a Claude Code session like this one (in the VS Code
  extension) has no "share link" feature. The conversation lives locally on your
  machine, not as a shareable page the way chats on claude.ai do. There are two
  ways to get it out.

  1. Export it with /export. Type /export in the Claude Code prompt. It saves
  the conversation to a text file or copies it to your clipboard. From there you
  can convert it to PDF, for example by pasting it into a doc and saving as
  PDF. That matters because the project spec asks for the AI-tool files as PDFs,
  not links.

  One caveat: this session was compacted partway through, when the context
  filled up. The export may include only the summary for the earlier part rather
  than every message.

  2. Use the full raw transcript. Claude Code keeps the complete session,
  including everything from before the compaction, as a JSON Lines file:

  ~/.claude/projects/-Users-awhdes-Documents-NUS-Masters-CS5424-Distributed-Data
  bases-citus-project/a55c4c04-838e-4a33-886f-eba22e511937.jsonl

  It isn't pleasant to read as is, since each line is one JSON message, but it
  is the complete record.

  If you'd rather have an actual link, I can turn the conversation into a
  readable page and publish it as a private artifact that only you can open
  until you choose to share it. Since it's your conversation, I'd only do that
  if you ask.
